942. GEORGE TAYLOR, JOHN MORRELL , the said JOHN SIBLEY, MATTHEW HAWES, SAMUEL DAWKINS , and RICHARD ANGLE , were indicted for stealing 8 trusses of hay, value 10 s., the goods of William Larman the younger, the master of Taylor, Sibley, Mor-rell, Hawes, and Dawkins.—2nd Count, stating them to be the goods of Richard Dixon, the master of Angle.
WILLIAM LAEMAN, JUN . I am a hay-dealer, living at Enfield; Taylor, Sibley, Morrell, Hawes, and Dawkins were in my employ. Each of them drove a load of hay to Whitechapel-market on the morning of the 20th of March, to sell for me—I know of their all having to take hay of mine to Mr. Dixon that morning—they had to take four loads of meadow hay, three loads of clover, and one of cinquefoil.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Do you remember on that morning,' Taylor, Morrell, and Sibley being there? A. Yes—I drove in a little before nine o'clock, and got the notes—I gave Sibley the note for the four loads of meadow hay, and the other note to Taylor.
ROBERTSON SWEETING . I am salesman to Mr. Larman. I remember selling four loads of meadow hay, and some other hay, for him to Mr. Dixon on the morning of the 23rd of March—Taylor, Sibley, Morrell, Hawes, and Dawkins conveyed the loads that morning—this is the ticket I gave.
HENRY OVENALL . I am thirteen years old; I am in the service of Mr. Larman. On the morning of the 20th of March I went with a load of my master's hay to Whitechapel-market—Taylor, Sihley, Morrell Hawes, and Dawkins all drove loads of hay to Whitechapel-market that morning—I was in Whitechapel market when four loads of meadow-hay were ordered to he taken to Mr. Dixon's—Hawes, Sibley, Dawkins, and I went with these four loads—we went round by Mr. Banks's, and after we had stopped there we went on to Mr. Dixon's.
COURT. Q. How much did you start with from Mr. Larman's? A. A load apiece, each load containing thirty-six trusses—before we arrived at Mr. Dixon's there had been three trusses a piece taken off two of the loads, and two trusses apiece off the two other loads—they were ten trusses deficient in all.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Were all the trusses, except those ten, delivered at Mr. Dixon's? A. No; one truss was brought away in my cart—Morrell, Dawkins, and Taylor helped to unload my cart—I saw Angle about the yard, I never saw him touch any hay—I did not drive my cart down Mr. Dixon's yard—I minded Dawkins's cart while he unloaded mine—I saw my cart when it had come out of the yard—there was one truss of hay in it, similar to what I had taken there—my cart went down to the Wagon and Horses—I and Dawkins went with it—I saw Angle in Mr. Dixon's yard when I came out—he was ever so far off the cart—I never saw any cart come out of the yard—T was minding Dawkins's cart in Barbican—I was standing ever so far from the gateway, a good bit farther than the length of this Court—while my cart was in the yard I went in and done the ropes up after they had undone them—I did up Dawkins's ropes when they had taken them off, and then he unloaded his cart, and then he undid mine—I drew my cart up the yard, then he unloaded his, and then I went out with his cart—I staid while they began to untie mine—Dawkins, Taylor, and Morrell were there, and Angle was up and down the yard—I was there while Dawkins's cart was being unloaded, and let him unload my cart as well as his own—when we got to the Wagon and Horses the truss of hay which was in my cart was put into the stable there—Angle came to town that morning by one of the carts from Tottenham—when we were at the Wagon and Hones Dawkins asked me for 1 s., which I gave him; and as we were going along the road home he told me he had given Dick 2 s. 9 d.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You took the hay to Mr. Banks to steal it? A. Yes, I knew what I was going there for—I did not know that it was arranged to carry off a truss in each cart when we got to Mr. Dixon's yard, nor that three trusses were to come out—I only knew of one truss coming out—I got nothing for that truss which was carried out of the yard—I got 3 s. in all—I had 4 s., and I gave Dawkins 1 s. back at the Wagon and Horses—I sold two trusses of my master's bay at Mr. Banks's for that 3 s.—I was not surprised to see my trusses walk off at Mr. Banks's, but 1 did not know where they were going—I have nener been in any scrape, or in custody—I was not to get anything if I came as a witness that I know of—I did not know that I should not be tried if I came as a witness, nor think I should escape if I came here to betray my comrades—I saw Angel coming from Tottenham that morning—I saw him just before I got up Tottenham High Cross hill—I did not speak to him—I recollected that I saw him when I was examined at the police-court.
COURT. Q. Where did Angle get down? A. Just as we got on the stones—he rode from the top of Stamford-hill on Sibley's cart—he rode three or four miles.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Taylor and Morrell were not with you when you went to Banks's? A. No—I taw them after we got to Dixon's, and I had seen them in the market—I saw them helping to onload the carts at Dixon's, and Shepperd was tbfre, bat I dick not see him at Banks's—there were eight carts in all—Hawes and Sibley and Daw-kins and I went first to Mr. Dixon's. and then Taylor, Morrtll, Shepperd, and my brother came there—when Hawes and Sibley had unloaded they went away—I saw Taylor and Morrel's carts in the street—Shepperd and toy brother were attending to them.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Whom did you tell first what you had been doing? A. Mr. Taylor the officer, at Mr. Larman's house the same night.
WILLIAM MEADING . I was working as a bricklayer at Mr. Dixota's that morning. I remember helping to unload two carts that had meadow hay upon them—Sibley had the care of one of the carts—I did not know the man with had the care of the other—when the hay was taktn oat, one truss was left in each of the two carts which were driven by Sibley and the other man—I do not think either of those two carts was driven by Ovenall—the driver went away with those trussee—Angle was in the yard when the carts turned round and went oat, and be followed them—I saw Taylor, Sibley, Morrell, Dawkim, and Hawes there, but I cannot tell which carts they were with—after I had helped ta tske in this hay, and Angle had followed the carts, I went after him-and told htm there were eight trusses short—I said there were three short in each cart, and one trass in each cart had been taken away—he said, "Very well, it is all right"—I took in thirty-two trusses from each of those carts into the loft.
COURT. Q. Was Angle by when you took them in? A. He was in the yard—when the carts went out he could see the trusses in them—the carts went to the Black Horse, tnd Angle went there.
MR. BODKIN. Q. And were the drivers there? A. Sibley was there and the other driver, whom I do not know—they drank together, and asked me to drink—I taw Sibley give Angle 2 s., and the other carter gate Angle 2 s.—Angle said to me, speaking of Sibley's cart, "You will see the nest where I rode up in the morning"—in the evening Angle said, "I got 8 s. 6 d."—he gave me 5 s. to be between me and Garrett—I gave Garrett 2 s., and was to have ghfta him the other 6 d., but I had not got it.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You knew they were all robbing their master, and you were to share the plunder? A. it—I did not know it till I found the quantity hay deficient.
Q. Do you mean to say you have ttot done it half a dozen times, or that this is the first time? A. It is not often I take the hey in—it hat been going on some time, and I have joined in it—I cannot exactly say whether Angle was loading the cart for Finchley while I was receiving the hay into the loft.
ALFRED SEKRS . I am clerk to Mr. Dixon. On the 30th of March Angle handed this ticket in through the rails that go round my desk, and said, "Four loads of hay, Sweeting"—I understood by that that he had received four loads of hay, and upon that I gave him this other ticket.
WILLIAM LARMAN re-examined. I received this ticket, acknowledging the receipt of the four loads of hay from Sibley at home, and this other is the ticket I gave him in the morning at market—these prisoners and Shepperd were all sent with hay to Mr. Dixon's.
SAMUEL TAYLOR . I am constable of Whitechapel-market. I took all the prisoners into custody—Dawkins said to me, "It is a bad job, Master Taylor, I have been drawn into this by Taylor and others"—he said, after unloading at Dixon's he had been to a public-house, and he had given Angle half-a-crown and a pot of beer.
(Angle received a pood character.)
TAYLOR, MORRELL, HAWES, and DAWKINS — NOT GUILTY .
SIBLEY — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Nine Months.
ANGLE — GUILTY. Aged 41,— Judgment Respited.—(See page 939.)
First Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.

943. ALEXANDER MCCOY and CHARLES MCCOY were indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 13 bottles, value 3 s.; 1 gallon of ale, 4 s.; and 6 pints of stout, 3 s.; the goods of Arthur Rumsey, their master, and that Alexander McCoy had been before convicted of felony.
ARTHUR RUMSEY . I am a wine-merchant, and live in Old Bond-street. Alexander M'Coy was in my employ for about a month or six weeks, and Charles, only on the day on which he was taken into custody. In consequence of some suspicion, 1 directed the police to watch my premises—I cannot swear to this hamper, but I can to the stout and ale it contains—the bottles have my name on them—the prisoners had no authority to remove them from my premises.
Alexander M'Coy. Q. Did you not give me authority to remove everything from your premises? A. You were to remove ale and stout from the cellar in Barlow-mews to the cellar in Bond-street.
ARTHUR GORE ( police-constable D 199.) In consequence of information from Mr. Rumsey, I watched his wine-cellar in Barlow-mews, on the 22nd, 23rd, and 24th of March—on Monday, the 24th, about half-past twelve o'clock in the day, I saw Alexander M'Coy bring this hamper from the cellar, and place it convenient to the door—he hoisted it on the witness Coram's shoulder—I saw Charles M'Coy standing at the door, keeping it partly ajar, while Alexander was looking round—I followed Coram, and stopped him in Regent-street—he banded me this ticket—I took him to the station with this hamper—it contains seven bottles of ale and six of stout.
Alexander M'Coy. Q. Did you see me from a public-house window? A. Yes—Barlow-mews is near Bruton-street—it runs into Grosvenor-square—Regent-street would be in the way from Barlow-mews to Old Bond-street—Coram had crossed it.
THOMAS CORAM . I am a jobbing-porter in Bruton-street, and have been there thirteen years, employed by different families. On Monday, the 24th of March, Alexander M'Coy engaged me to take this hamper to Marshall-street, Silver-street, Golden-square, to the first green-grocer's—I was to receive 6 d. when I got there—I was going with it there, and was stopped by the policeman at the corner of Regent-street and New Burlington-street—the paper I gave him was given me by Charles M'Coy, at the time I had the hamper given me, to give to the person at the place.
Alexander M'Coy. Q. Did I give you the hamper? A. I cannot be sure which one it was—it was at the bottom of the stairs that it was put on my shoulder—I did not touch it till then—I was at the top of the Mews, talking to another porter, when you hired me—I asked you to lend me 1 d.—I did not leave you at the top of the mews.
COURT. Q. Is Marshall-street in the way to Old Bond-street, from Barlow-mews? A. No, quite away from it.
Alexander M'Coy's Defence. I was employed to bottle, and move some stout and ale from Barlow-mews; on Saturday Mr. Ramsey ordered me to get a man to assist me, and I got my brother on Monday morning, and set him to washing bottles; I had authority to move everything out of thje place; and Mr. Rumsey particularly ordered me to keep the cellar clean; I had a hamper there with the sweepings of the cellar, and broken glass, as the ostlers in the mews would not allow me to throw it on the dung-heap; I had packed up several hampers; I asked this man to take this hamper away for me; he said he would; he went into tbepafclk-house, J believe, to speak to the policeman, came out again, and while I was at the top of the mews, he took this hamper; I met him at the top of the mews; his hat had fallen off; I put it on; he seemed very much confused; I did not notice it was not the right hamper, as I had lots of hampers, dowi there ready filled to be taken away; my brother knows nothing about it.
Charles M'Coy's Defence. I was washing bottles when Coram came and said, "Is this the hamper? I said, "I don't know;" he took it from under the straw, and went up stairs; my brother was not in the cellar at the time.
MR. RUMSEY re-examined. Alexander had no authority to employ a porter.
JOHN COPUS ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of Alexander McCoy's former conviction—( read—Convicted 28th June, 7th Vict., by the name of Thomas Jones, of stealing 24 bottles of ale, confined One Month)—he is the person.
Prisoner. I plead guilty to that.
ALEX. MCCOY — GUILTY . Aged 33— Transported for Seven Years. CHARLES MCCOY — NOT GUILTY .

944. JAMES PIDDINGTON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of March, 2 blankets, value 6 s.; 1 sheet, 2 s.; and 1 umbrella, 2 s.; the goods of William Piddington: and GEORGE THOMAS PEARSON for feloniously receiving 1 blanket, well knowing it to have been stolen.
WILLIAM FREEMAN . I live in Suffblfr-street, Clerkenwell. I stopped Piddington at Smithfield-bars, on Easter Monday morning, at a quarter to eight o'clock, with a bundle under his arm, and an umbrella—there warn a sheet and blanket in the bundle—I asked him if they belonged to my mother—he said, no, they did not, and offered me the bundle—several drovers took him away from me, and he ran towards Charter-house-aquaie—I ran after him, calling "Stop thief"—he dropped the bundle—I took it up, and saw him stopped, without losing sight of him—the policeman came up, and I gave him the bundlfeftad the prisoner—this is the bundle—I knew the prisoner before. HENRY TODD ( policeman.) The prisoner was given into my custody by Freeman, with these articles.
WILLIAM PIDDINGTON . I am the prisoner Piddington's father, and live in Hemming's-buildings, Chapel-street, Clerkenwell. I took him to lodge with me—he got up on Easter Monday morning, and took three blankets and a sheet from his bed, and an umbrella—these produced are mine, and were safe when I went to bed on Sunday night—they were gone when I
got up at a quarter past seven, and the street door left open—I know Pearson to be an associate of my son's—he lives in White Lion-street, about twenty yards from me—I have seen him about my house, but not on this morning.
HENRY DOUGHTY . I am in the service of Mr. Rosier, a pawnbroker, in Clerkenwell. I produce a blanket pawned by Pearson, for 2 s., on the morning the policeman came.
Pearson. Q. How do you know I pledged it? A. I was there at the time—it was early in the morning.
BENJAMIN PHILLIPS ( policeman.) I apprehended Pearson on the Monday evening—I told him he had received a blanket from Piddington, and pledged it—he said yes, he had, he had lost the ticket and spent the money.
Piddington put in a petition, stating himself alone to be guilty; that he gave the blanket to Pearson to pledge, to pay him 2 s. which he owed, and that his father had been very cruel to him.
Pearson's Defence. I acknowledge pawning the blanket, but I did not know it was stolen.
PIDDINGTON — GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Four Months.
PEARSON — NOT GUILTY .

945. CHARLES MADDEN and JOHN BRUMMELL were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of Jan., 1 cloak, value 15 s., the goods of William Smith; and that Madden had been before convicted of felony.
CHARLES RANDALL ( police-constable N 172.) On the 7th of Jan., about five o'clock in the afternoon, I was in company with Withers, and met the two prisoners together in Upper-street, Islington—we followed, and lost sight of them for about five minutes—we were then standing in the Liverpool-road, and saw Brummell coming towards us, on the same side as we were—directly he saw me he started back a pace or two—I then saw Madden close behind him—Bnrannell tried to hide him—as they crossed the road I saw Madden had something under his arm—I pursued them—Brummell saw I was running after him, and turned to the left-Madden turned, saw me coming, and dropped something which he bad under his arm—I did not stop to pick it up, but as I ran by it I saw it appeared to be a doak—I still kept after him—I could not see which house he went into, he escaped at that time, and was not taken till the 23rd of March—directly he was taken I went to Brummell's house, and told him Madden was taken for stealing the cloak—he said, "You might have taken me before if you liked; I saw you on Friday; I thought it had blown over by this time."
THOMAS WITHERS ( police-constable N 211.) I was with Randall on the evening of the 7th of Jan., and saw the prisoners together in Upper-street, Islington—a few minutes afterwards I saw Madden with a bundle, and saw him drop it—I was about twenty yards off—I picked it up—it was this cloak.
JAMES WAREHAM . I am in the service of William Smith, a pawnbroker, at the corner of the Liverpool-road, Islington. This cloak is his property—I hung it at the door on the morning in question, but did not miss it till the next morning.
Madden's Defence. He never saw me about the shop; the policeman tells a falsehood; I never saw the cloak.
Brumnell's Defence. If I had been there, and close to Madden, could the other constable be off seeing me?
THOMAS WITHERS . I produce a certificate of Madden's former conviction from this Court—( read—Convicted 21th Nov., 1843, of larceny, after a previous conviction, and confined one year.)—I was present at his trial—he is the person—I know him perfectly well.
MADDEN — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Seven Years.
BRUMMELL — GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Six Months.

946. JOHN RANDALL was indicted for bigamy.
FRANCES ATLEE . I am single, and live at Barewood, Berks. On the 27th of March, 1837, I was present at Ealing church, Middlesex, at a marriage between the prisoner and Mary Piggot—I have seen her here to-day.
Prisoner. Q. How many times did you see me? A. Never before or since—I am sure you are the person—my father was the clerk, and I was one of the witnesses.
MARY ANN UNDERWOOD . I am a widow, and live at No. 28, Tysson-street, Bethnal-green. I became acquainted with the prisoner about five years ago—he represented himself as single—I was married to him at Paddington church, by banns, on the 27th of Nov., 1841.
Prisoner. She was indebted to her landlord 30 s. for rent when I mar-ried her, so I could not marry her for her property. Witness. It is false—I did not owe my landlord anything—he has robbed me a great deal.
WILLIAM COLEMAN ( police-sergeant.) I produce two extracts, which were made in my presence, from the parish registers of Ealing and Padding-ton—I compared them both with the originals, and they corresponded—I saw the clergymen sign them—( the extracts were here read.)
Prisoner's Defence. My first wife absconded from me, and was away seven years and nine months; I never could learn whether she was living or dead.
GUILTY . Aged 54.— Confined Twelve Months.

JAMES KING ( policeman.) On Saturday night, the 29th of March. about half-past ten o'clock, I was on duty in Crown-lane, North-end, Fulham, and saw the prisoner standing about twelve yards from Mr. Orchard's shop—I watched him, and saw him crawl on his hands and knees about twelve yards, to the shop door, take a piece of beef, and crawl back in the same position—he went about fifty yards—I went after him—when he saw me coming close to him he threw the beef down, and ran away—he ran about a mile before I caught him but I was not above a yard from him all the way.
WILLIAM JAMES ORCHARD . I am a butcher, living at North-end. Fulham. King brought me a piece of beef on Saturday night, which I had put on the block about half an hour before—I missed it just as he came in with it.
Prisoners Defence. I was in liquor, or I should not have done it, and I did not know I had done it till the policeman told me.
JAMES KING re-examined. He was not in liquor—he ran very fast for a mile.
ALEXANDER MCCASKERY ( policeman.) I produce a certificate from this Court of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted 24th October, 1842, of larceny; after a previous conviction; confined one year)—I was present at both the trials, and know the prisoner to be the person.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.

949. TURNER PERKISS was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 34 dishes, value 17 s.; 33 plates, 16 s.; 4 butter-boats, 2 s.; 1 cup, 3 d.; 1 saucer, 3 d.; 12 knives, 18 s.; and 24 forks, 12 s.; the goods of Samuel Lovegrove and others, his masters: and FRANCES SALES , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
JOHN GEARING ( police-constable N 328.) On Tuesday, the 8th of April, I searched Sale's house at Chingford, in Essex, and there found the articles stated—the greater portion were in a box in a bed-room—it was not locked—no person was in the house—I know it to be Sales—I have known her five years—I afterwards went to Woodford, and found Sales at the house of a Mrs. Witherby, washing—I took her into custody, took her to Chingford, took the articles out of the box, showed them to her, and asked if that was the property I had alluded to on the road—she said that was the property, but it was not hers, it was her brother's, and he had sent them to her to take care of—I asked where her brother lived—she said she did not know, he had gone travelling—I asked his name—she said, "Perkiss"—she afterwards said his name was Turner Perkiss, and that he lived at No. 15, Cold-harbour, Blackwall—I went to Blackwall that evening, and from inquiries, went to the West India Dock tavern, saw Mr. Lovegrove, and showed him some of the things—he called Perkiss in—I showed him the portion of the property I had brought with me, and
asked if he knew it, and if he had at any time sent any portion of that property to a Mrs. Sales, at Chingford to be taken care of—he said he hid not—I asked him about Mrs. Sales being his sister—he said she was—I searched the house in Cold-harbour next morning, but found nothing.
LEWIS LOVEGROVE . I have the management of the West India Dock tavern, for Samuel Lovegrove and others, the trustees of the late Samuel Lovegrove—Perkiss frtfs been a servant there for about three years, and in the family a number of years—I have examined all these articles in the box—they are the property, of, Samuel Lovegrove and others—I know them well—the china has our private mark on it, "W. I. D. T.," for West India Dock Tavern—Perkiss had no authority to take them away—the knives and forks are such as we had in use.
Perkiss's Defence. The knives and forks were never on the premises, and were never Mr. Lovegrove's property—the plates and things were taken from the rubbish-hole, where they had been thrown.
Sale's Defence. I did not know they were stolen.
MR. LOVEGROVE re-examined. The things were not thrown away—many of them have not the least damage or chip.
NOT GUILTY .

I left home about half-past five o'clock—the door which is in two halves was fastened and bolted, and the windows safe—they do not open—I returned at ten at night, found the top part of the door broken open, the bolt wrenched completely off, and only hanging by part of the screws—I missed a book, a trinket case, a cigar case, brass tube and pipe, which were safe when I went out—I have seen nothing but the book since—this now produced is it—it is the "Builder", and is my own binding.

ALEXANDER MOFFATT . I am an apprentice belonging to the Tory. The prisoner had no business on board and was ordered off—as he was going over the side a constable stopped him with a bag belonging to the ship—it contained this jacket, and a mat belonging to the ship—Edward Duncan is the captain—the things were in his care.
JOHN JONES . I am a watchman in St. Katherine's Dock. On the afternoon of the 3rd of April, I was called on board the Tory to turn the prisoner ashore—I stopped him with this bag—I asked if it belonged to him—he said, yes—it contained the jacket and mat—I asked if he put them in—he said, no, he knew nothing about it.
( The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that the mate had allowed him to take some rubbish, and he put the articles in the bag thinking them of no value, that it was not likely he should steal them, knowing he must be searched on leaving the Dock.)
JOHN JONES re-examined. Labourers are searched when they leave the docks, and are stopped if they have property in their possession not their own—I have seen the prisoner in the Docks.

NOT GUILTY .

957. JOHN HEATH, WILLIAM CAFFYN , and JOHN CAFFYN , were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of April, at St. Leonard's, Shoreditch, 63lbs. weight of tea, value 18 l.; and 1 tea-chest, 1 s.; the goods of John Bagley, in his dwelling-house; to which
HEATH pleaded GUILTY . Aged 27, received a good character. Confined Twelve Months.
JOHN BAGLEY . I am a grocer, and live in Haberdasher-walk, in the parish of St. Leonard Shoreditch. It is my dwelling-house—this teachest produced is mine, and when I lost it was full of tea, and worth more than 18 l.—it had never been opened—I saw it safe on the 7th of April, about twenty minutes past nine o'clock at night, within the cill of the shop-door, on a chest of congou—I did not see it taken, but I missed it in a very few minutes—people came round the door, and said, " A chest of tea is gone"—I left the shop and ran across the road, and saw the chest of tea lying on the ground, and Heath in the policeman's custody—I did not see either of the other prisoners at that time, but have no hesitation in saying that I saw John Caffyn looking in at my window about three quarters of an hour before the tea was stolen.
JOHN NEWELL . I am a policeman. About five minutes past nine o'clock on the evening of the 7th of April, I saw Heath standing some few yards from Mr. Bagley's shop, and also saw William Caffyn and a man resembling John, but of him I am not positive—I am positive of William—I knew him previously—I saw him looking in at Mr. Bagley's shop window—I did not notice him any time there—suspecting what they were about, I asked Sergeant Boyd to watch them for a short time, while I went to change my clothes—the two went away, leaving Heath in the street, whom I did not then suspect—I changed my clothes, came back, and followed them a little distance—I saw them run up Myrtle-street, near Hoxton-church—I lost sight of them for some little time—I then went back to the shop, and found all three together close to the prosecutor's shop, looking in at the window—they crossed and recrossed the street two or three times—I then saw William Caffyn go and stand at Mr. Bagley's private door, and Heath and the other were on the opposite side of the way—William Caffyn crossed to them, and all three were together again—I then saw Heath go to Mr. Bagley's private door—after watching him some time, I saw him put his foot on the cill of the shop, and take the chest of tea—he crossed to Buttesland-street, and went some distance up the street—I followed him—William Caffyn and the third man walked towards him—as I went towards Heath he dropped the chest of tea, and was about to run away when I made a jump at him, and seized him—the other two got away—I knew where William Caffyn lived—I went to his lodging, and apprehended him—he said he had not been in Hoxton that night—I am positive he is the person I saw with Heath—I should be sorry to swear to John—he is like the third person.
JOHN WILLIAM BOYD ( police-sergeant.) About half-past seven o'clock on this Monday evening I saw the two Caffyns—I did not know them previously, but I bad such a sight of them as to be able to swear to them—they were looking in at Mr. Bagley's shop—I saw them again about seven or ten minutet past nine, looking in at Mr. Bagley's shop again—I gave a description of John Caffyn's person to the inspector on duty at the station, and next morning on his coming with his brother's breakfast, he was detained till I came—the inspector asked if there was any one there, I knew—I pointed to John Caffyn, and said "That is the man"—I am positive he is the man.
William Caffyn's Defence. I was at home helping my father, who was very busy that night, and was not out at all that night; I had my supper and was in bed by half-past ten o'clock; my brother is quite innocent.
John Caffyn's Defence. On Monday night I went out and met a
young man who had got a benefit, and asked me to take a ticket at 3 d., as he had had no work for a long time; I said I would, and I was there from half-past eight o'clock till the clock struck eleven, and never left the room; I then went to bed; last week I had five or six witnesses to prove where I was at the time I am accused of; when the policeman came to take me he put his lantern in my face, and said "I think you are one;" my father said, "No, I know he is not, because he shewed me a ticket where he was last night;" it is not likely if I was guilty, I should have brought my brother's breakfast next morning to the station house.
MARY DAVIS . My husband is a vellum-binder, and lives in Brunswick-place, Backchurch-lane—I know John Caffyn was in Brick-lane from half-past five till turned half-past eight on the Monday evening—he was in the lane, and had got a toasting-fork which he had bought to take home to his mother—I had his little brother, five years old, at my place, to mind, I took him home, and was coming back when I saw him in the lane—that was about five o'clock, and it was about half-past eight or a quarter to nine o'clock when I saw him with the toasting-fork—he was not in the lane three hours—he was doing nothing at five o'clock—he merely said, "There you are, Mrs. Davis," and I said, "There you are"—he was walking along the street—I said, "Is your mother at home," and he said, "Yes, she is not very well"—I did not see him all the time between a quarter past five and half-past eight o'clock—I cannot tell what he was doing in the mean time.
JOHN COX . I am a labourer and live in Boundary-street, at the back of Shoreditch church. I was in company with John Caffyn on Monday afternoon, this day week, and was with him at a public-house, at the corner of Booth-street, Brick-lane, from eight o'clock in the evening till ten—I do not know the sign of the house, or the landlord's name—I do not often go there—we were up stairs—there was a set-to, sparring, for the benefit of a young man—I dare say there were fifty people there—the room was full, but no one else that I knew—we had a drop of porter—there was no one with him but me.
JOHN NEWELL re-examined. Brick-lane is about twenty minutes' walk from the prosecutor's, and Booth-street about a quarter of an hour's walk.
PETER CAFFYN . I am the prisoner's father, and am a chair-maker, living in Epsworth-court, Virginia-row—William was at home by half-past ten, and John by half-past twelve o'clock—he was at the Barley-mow, in Brick-lane, till then—I did not see him there.
JOHN NEWELL re-examined. I swear I saw William Caffyn and Heath together, not two minutes before the tea was taken—I have some doubt about John.
JOHN WILLIAM BOYD re-examined. I took particular notice of both the Caffyns' and swear I saw them at the shop about the time the tea was taken.
WILLIAM CAFFYN — GUILTY.* Aged 19.
JOHN CAFFYN — GUILTY . Aged 24.
Confined Twelve Months.

958. MARY ANN BIRCH was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Cooper about ten in the night of the 7th of April, at St. Pancras, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 pair of sugar-tongs, value 5 s.; and 1/4lb. of tea, 1 s.; his property: and 1 tea-chest, 15 s.; 1 sugar-basin, 5 s.; the goods of William Cooper, the younger.
WILLIAM COOPER , jun. I am the son of William Cooper, a piano-forte maker, No. 24, William-street, Hampstead-road, in the parish of St. Pancras—it is his dwelling-house—I reside with him—the prisoner used to come as a charwoman, and was discharged last July—after she left I missed the key of our street door—on the night of the 7th April, about a quarter past nine o'clock, my brother Thomas went out—I saw him leave the house, and am confident he shut the street door after him—I did not see him do so, but I was on the stairs and distinctly heard the door shut—it has a peculiar sound when it shuts—there is no getting in at the window, the area is wide—the windows were all down, and I believe fastened—about a quarter of an hour afterwards I was in the kitchen and distinctly heard a key put in the street door, the door opened, and then shut to—after that I heard a noise in the parlour like a person tumbling over the chain—I came up from the kitchen and went into the passage with a light—I listened for a minute, then went to open the door, and it was put to directly—in half a minute it opened and the prisoner came out with the tea-chest on her arm—I pushed her back into the parlonr, locked the door, and locked her in—I fetched a constable and she was taken—when I went in, the tea-chest was in its usual place on the sideboard—when I returned from the station in about twenty minutes, I examined and found the books and papers and ink-stand thrown on the sofa, and the ink spilled on the sofa, and the table-cover all bundled up in a heap on the table—under the sofa I saw the policeman find this key—it is the key of the street door which we had lost—I know it by its being broken—this tea-chest and sugar-basin are mine, the tongs are my father's.
MATTHEW HUTCHINSON ( police-sergeant S 124.) On the 7th of April, about half-past nine at night, I was called to Mr. Cooper's—I found the prisoner in the parlour—she was given ipto my custody—she said nothing—I took her to the station—she was Searched there by a female who gave me this key—this other key I picked up under the soil—it opens the prosecutor's street door—the other will not.
Prisoner's Defence. On Saturday I saw Mr. Cooper, sen., and asked him to lend me two shillings; he said he had not got it in his pocket, but if I would see him at the corner of William-street on Monday sight he would give it me; I went and walked as far as the door; it was ajar; I looked in to the parlour, and the prosecutor ran to the door and shut me in; I never saw the caddy till I was at the office; Mr. Cooper, sen., has often been to my house and given me money as a friend.
GUILTY.* —Aged 35. Transported for Ten Years.
NEW COURT.— Tuesday, April 15, 1845.
Sixth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.

960. HENRY GROOM was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
ANN ELLIOTT . I am a widow, and keep a chandler's shop in Well-street, Mile-end, New Town. On the 5th of April the prisoner came to the shop in the
evening for half an ounce of common tobacco, which came to three farthings—he gave me a sixpence—I gave him five pence farthing—he went away—I put the six pence into a basin in which there was only two four-penny pieces beside—I am quite certain of that—my daughter came in about two minutes after, and from what she said I went to the basin—I found in it only the two four-penny pieces and the sixpence—no one had been in the shop—on Sunday, the 6th of April, the prisoner came again—I was in the shop alone—I remembered him again—he asked for a quartern loaf and some other things, which came to sixpence—he put his hand into his pocket and took out a shilling—my daughter had then come into the shop and she took up the shilling and said, "You gave my mother a bad sixpence last evening"—he said he did not, and he had not been in the shop, but I have no doubt of him whatever.
SARAH ANN GREEN . I am Mrs. Elliott's daughter. About seven o'clock on Saturday evening, the 5th of April, I was in the parlour—there is a window between that and the shop—I saw the prisoner come in, and took notice enough of him to know him again—about two minutes after he was gone I looked at the sixpence in the basin and found it was bad—I put it apart from other money till next day, when the prisoner came again—I knew him to be the same person—I am confident of him—he bought some things of my mother, which came to sixpence, and put down a shilling—I took it up and found it was bad—I told him so, and I told him he had been there the day before—he said he had not, that he had taken the shilling of a young man for some work, and would fetch him—I told him to go and he left the shop—I went a short distance after him—he turned up Booth-street—I saw a constable and directed him to take him—I gave the constable the bad shilling and sixpence.
MICHAEL MAHAN ( police-constable H 79.) I took the prisoner, and found on him two good sixpences and 1 s. 3 1/2 d. in copper—I received this bad shilling and sixpence from Green.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. This sixpence and shilling are both counterfeit.
Prisoner's Defence. On Saturday evening I was not near the shop; I was selling books about seven o'clock.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18450407-961]
961. GEORGE TAYLOR, JOHN MORRELL , and JOHN SHEPHERD were indicted for stealing 4 trusses of hay, value 12 s., the goods of William Larman, the younger, their master; RICHARD ANGLE , for feloniously harbouring the said prisoners; and WILLIAM BANKS , for feloniously receiving the said hay, well knowing it to have been stolen.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of Richard Dixon.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM LARMAN, JUN . I reside at Enfield, and am in the habit of sending hay to Whitechapel-market—Taylor, Morrell, and Shepherd were carmen of mine. On the 20th of March I sent by them several carts to London loaded with hay—they started about two o'clock in the morning—Taylor took a load of clover hay—Morrell had a load of cinqfoil—Shepherd and James Ovenall had each a load of clover bay—I went up to Whitechapel-market, and got there a little before nine o'clock—I found the carts all there, and apparently all rightly loaded, with thirty-six trusses in
each load—this ticket was given me when I got there—it is a ticket of Mr. Sweeting's, my hay salesman, and indicates that Mr. Dixon, of Barbican, had bought three loads of clover and one of cinqfoil—I gave this ticket to Taylor to take there, and it was his duty to bring me back a counter ticket, acknowledging the receipt of the hay at Mr. Dixon's—I then sent off the four loads referred to in this ticket to go to Mr. Dixon's, and they went out of the market together—I think Taylor's cart went first, but I am not quite certain—it was their duty to go directly to Mr. Dixon's—Brick-lane would be their way to go if they went by Spitalfields church—Banks's shop is in Brick-lane, but it is some distance beyond where they ought to have turned to go by Spitalfields church—his shop is out of their way—some information reached me, and I went to Banks's shop, about ten o'clock or a few minutes after—I found one of my carts which had the load of cinqfoil, which Morrell had the charge of, standing at Banks's side door, and Banks was standing by the tide, of it—directly Banks saw me he made away into his shop—I went up to Morrell, and asked him what he wanted with his cart there—he looked down on the ground, and said they had been leaving a truss of hay a-piece.
Q. Did he say they or we have been leaving a truss of hay a-piece, or "I have been putting a truss of clover in at Banks's?" A. He said, "We have been leaving a truss of hay a-piece"—I am sure he said "a-piece"—Banks heard that—he was not two yards off me—I told Morrell I should take him before a Magistrate—he then drove on—Banks went in—I went in, and spoke to him about receiving hay of mine, and he begged for mercy—I did not see any clover or cinqfoil hay in his shop at that time, but I saw some meadow hay which I knew to be mine—I went to the market, and then went back to Banks's with the constable—I did not take a cart with me, but one came afterwards—I looked in a back place at Banks's, and saw three trusses of clover and a truss of cinqfoil, which I know to be mine, and to be part of that which had been sold to Mr. Dixon, and which is referred to in this note—I received the other ticket between eleven and twelve o'clock that day from Charles Taylor.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Just recollect yourself, was not what Morrell said when you asked him what he did there, "I have been putting a truss of clover hay a-piece off each cart at Mr. Banks's?" A. Yes.
COURT. Q. Did he say, "They hare been putting a truss of clover hay each," or "I have," or "We have?" A. "I have been putting a truss of clover bay a-piece."
ROBERTSON SWEETING . I am salesman at Whitecbapel for Mr. Larman—I sold these four loads for him to Mr. Dixon on the 20th of March—I gave this sale note to Mr. Larman.
JAMES OVENALL . I am fourteen years old; I have been in the employ of Mr. Larman since last harvest; I live with my mother, at Enfield Wash. On the 20th of March I went to town with a load of clover of my master's—there were as many as eleven carts of Mr. Larman's went to town that morning together—Taylor drove one of clover, Morrell one of cinqfoil, and Shepherd and I had each one of clover—we got to Whitechapel about six o'clock—after the hay was sold I saw a ticket given to Taylor—we all four left the market together—Taylor went first, and the other three followed—we went down into Spitalfields—I do not know the name of the street—all four stopped there—two trusses of hay were taken off Taylor's cart, two off Shepherd's, and two off mine—they were all put on Morrell's
cart—Taylor, Shepherd, and Morrell were all present at the time it was done—I do not know what was done with the trusses afterwards—Taylor's cart, and Shepherd's, and mine then went on, and unloaded at Mr. Dixon's—Morrell did not start to go there at the time we did, but I saw him coming after us all the way we went to Mr. Dixon's—Banks's shop is in Brick-lane—where we stopped was ever so far from Banks's shop—our carts went a little way up Brick-lane, and then turned off in the direction of Mr. Dixon's—Morrell overtook us almost as soon as we got to Mr. Dixon's—Taylor's cart and Shepherd's went first into Mr. Dixon's yard—I waited outside till they were both unloaded, and then I drove in—the hay was taken in at the loft at Mr. Dixon's—we then went home with our carts—we stopped at the Wagon and Horses, at Hoxton, and had our dinner—we had not stopped at any house before that—when we had got pretty well home that day I received 1 s. 6 d. from Morrell—he did not say what it was for—I thought it was for the clover, but nothing was said about it—I saw Morrell give Shepherd 1 s. 6 d. as he was going down Tottenham—Angle is in Mr. Dixon's employ—I saw him about the yard while we were unloading our carts—I saw him taking some straw in—I did not see him that morning, for I was riding.
COURT. Q. Could you see Morrell follow you all the way? A. Yes—he was a goodish bit behind us—when we got to Spitalfields, Taylor took two trusses of bay off his own cart, two off Shepherd's, and two off mine, and put them on Morrell's cart—I did not see any cart stop at Banks's—we were ever so far from Banks's.
RICHARD DIXON . I am the proprietor of the Repository, in Barbican: I am in the babit of purchasing hay of Mr. Larman, at Whitechapel. On the 20th of March I purchased three loads of clover, and one of cinqfoil—they are what are referred to in this note—according to our mode of doing business, this note would be brought by one of the carmen—I have a clerk named Seers, and the prisoner Angle was in my service—it was his duty to stand at the loft door, to count and weigh the hay that came in, and take the account of it to the counting-house with the salesman's ticket, and get another ticket, which he was to give to the carman—it was a ticket like this, ( looking at it,) acknowledging the receipt of the quantity—I think it was about one or two o'clock that day, that I heard something was wrong—I had seen Angle that morning after the hay had come, but before it was unloaded.
ALFRED SEERS . I am clerk to Mr. Dixon. This hay came from Whitechapel-market on the 20th of March—this sale-ticket was brought to me, but I do not recollect by whom—I gave this other note to Angle, and, according to the course of business, it was his duty to give this ticket, acknowledging the receipt of the hay, to one of the carmen who brought it, to take home to his employer.
WILLIAM MEADING . I was in the employ of Mr. Dixon on the 20th of March, as a bricklayer—I assisted in taking in hay from the carts that came that morning—I was up in the loft, and I saw Angle in the yard at the time the carts were unloading—after they were unloaded I went to the Black Horse public-house, which is close by Mr. Dixon's—I saw Angle there, and Sibley and another of the carters—I saw each of them give Angle 2 s., and he was drinking with them—I told him there were eight trusses of hay deficient, with the trusses they had taken back in their carts—he said, "Very well, it is all right."
CHARLES TAYLOR . I was in the employ of Mr. Larman. On the 20th of March I was at Whitechapel-market, and Mr. Larman gave me a message to take to Mr. Dixon's—I went there, and Mr. Basham gave me a ticket like this, but I cannot read—I gave it to my master.
JOHN BASHAM . I am clerk to Mr. Dixon. I remember Charles Taylor coming that morning with a message from Mr. Larman—I think it was between ten and eleven o'clock—I gave him this ticket, which I got from Angle, who was in the yard, taking in some straw—the last two carts were just about going out of the yard—I asked Angle what he had done with the ticket he had out of the counting-house—he said, "Here it is"—this refers to the clover-hay and cinqfoil—he took it out of his pocket—the carts which had left the meadow-hay were gone, the other carts were not—Angle did not give any reason why he had not given this ticket to the carman.
WILLIAM LARMAN re-examined. I got to Banks's that morning about ten o'clock—his house is in Brick-lane, nearly a quarter of a mile beyond the turning that should be taken to go to Mr. Dixon's—I did not see any of the carts but Morrell's—I sent Charles Taylor to Mr. Dixon's for the ticket, because I wanted to know whether my carts had left Mr. Dixon's, and if he met them he was to ask my men for the ticket—it was about eleven o'clock when I sent Charles Taylor—it was after Banks had been taken into custody, and after I had told Morrell that I would take him into custody—when I sent Charles Taylor, Morrell bad been gone from Banks's door about an hour—there was plenty of time for him to have got to Dixon's, and have told anybody there that I had found him at Banks's—I did not see George Taylor, the prisoner, again till he got home at night.
SAMUEL TAYLOR . I am constable of Whitechapel-market. I accompanied Mr. Larman to Banks's shop thai morning—I had seen these carts come into the market that morning, but I did not see them go out—I went to Banks's, and found three trusses of clover and one of cinqfoil—I took Banks into custody—I took George Taylor, Morrell, and Shepherd the same evening at a beer-shop at Enfield—they were not sober.
WILLIAM LARMAN re-examined. Morrell's cart was loaded with cinqfoil—he could not have got clover except from the other men—the clover I found in Banks's shop is what I sent by Taylor, Shepherd, and Ovenall—I could swear to it.
Cross-examined. Q. How do you know it? A. By the growing of it, and the tying of it—there was not much of that clover grown, for the first cut was almost all stubble—I cannot tell how many acres it grew upon, or how much there was of it—I had three loads of clover that day in the market, and they were all sold to Mr. Dixon—I had none then left at home—that was the last of the stack of clover-hay—there had been fourteen loads and six trusses in the stack, and that was the last of it.
(Morrell and Banks received good characters.)
MORRELL — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Nine Months.
BANKS — GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Six Months.
TAYLOR, SHEPHERD, and ANGLE — NOT GUILTY .

MR. CLARKSON offered no evidence.

963. RICHARD JOHNSON was indicted for assaulting Thomas Hirst, with intent, &c.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450407-964]
964. JOHN ELLARD, EDMUND AMER, JOHN MOORE , and ROBERT WALKER , were indicted for stealing 1 portmanteau, value 5 s.; 1 stock, 3 s. 6 d.; 1 neck-tie, 2 s.; 2 neckerchiefs, 1 s. 6 d.; 4 pairs of shoes, 12 s.; 1 pair of slippers, 2 s. 6 d.; 5 waistcoats, 2 l. 15 s.; 6 glass-cloths, 3 s.; 1 pair of gaiters, 5 s.; 1 handkerchief, 2 s. 6 d.; 1 pair of trowsers, 16 s.; 1 coat, 3 s.; and 2 scarfs, 10 s.; the goods of Samuel Pope; and that Walker had been before convicted of felony.
MR. BRIERLY conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL POPE . I am a valet. I am now lodging in Upper Springstreet—on the 25th of March I came from Chelmsford, and brought a portmanteau, containing a black stock, a coat, waistcoat, trowsers, and the other articles stated—I left the portmanteau at Mr. Wells's, the Spotted Dog, in the Strand, on Tuesday, the 25th of March—I went therefor it on the Saturday following, and Mr. Wells told me it had been stolen—I went to the station at Bow-street and saw my portmanteau there—this is it—the things that are here are a part of those that were in it—some of them are missing.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What did you leave them there for? A. I left them in charge of the landlady—I was on my way to Shropshire—they were to be left until I called for them—I left them in front of the bar—a person coming in would be able to reach them, but I saw many other things there, and I thought it might be a rule to leave them there—I left them about ten o'clock on Tuesday night, and did not go for them until Saturday morning—there was a name on the portmanteau, but there is not now—I know it, and I have the key of it—I have had it two or three years—here is the mark "Chelmsford" on it—I know the things—here are six glass-cloths, which have my name on them.
MR. BRIKRLY. Q. How far was your portmanteau from the door when you left it? A. Four or five yards, at the farther end of the shop.
WILLIAM WELLS . I am landlord of the Spotted Dog in the Strand. I know the persons of all the prisoners—I only know two of them by seeing them once or twice—the portmanteau was left at my house on the 25th of March'—I saw it safe between one and two o'clock in the morning on the 28th—the prisoners were there along with several more—they had something to drink, and got larking with each other—I looked round and missed several of the persons, and the portmanteau was gone—my house is a booking-office, and I keep it open in consequence of serving the persons from the Weekly Messenger and the Observer office—I have seen Moore once or twice.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNR. Q. Have you a distinct recollection of Amer being there? A. Yes, I have known him ever since I have kept the house—I am sure he was there, and he might have been there early in the evening—we have a great many come in—I do not take a very accurate note of what time they come in or go out—I should have had 2 d. for booking if I had kept this portmanteau safe—Amer bore a good character, to the best of my belief.
Cross-examined by MR. BALDWIN. Q. Do you know Walker? A.
Yes—he was there, to the best of my belief—I had not sufficient knowledge of him to know who he was then.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Have you known Ellard long? A. No—I recollect his being there, standing at the back of the engine, talking and laughing with the rest—there were about ten of them—they had half a pint of gin amongst them—there were about five there when I missed the portmanteau, and I asked them what had become of the rest—I was not aware that any of them were tipsy—Ellard might have been tipsy—I will swear he was in my place after twelve o'clock that night—he was there between one and two o'clock—I have seen him there on several occasions, and I have noticed when he came he took a particular stand against the engine.
Moore. Q. Can you swear I was there? A. To the best of my belief—if not, it was a person very much like you—I do not know that I should like to swear positively that you were.
MR. BRIERLY. Q. Which of them will you swear to? A. Amer and Ellard, and to the best of my belief Moore and Walker were there.
GEORGE TURK . I am a cab driver. On Friday morning, the 28th of March, I was the first cab on the rank near St. Clement's church, near the Spotted Dog—I was asleep, and was awoke by my cab door being opened, and two persons got in—two other persons got outside, and I was ordered to drive to Short's-gardens, Drury-lane—I went there, pulled up to a number, and received 1 s.—there was a portmanteau taken out of the cab, but I cannot say which of them had it in their hand—I got on my box, and turned round, and the portmanteau was taken into the house—I know Amer was one of the men—he is a driver at well as myself—he was one who was outside the cab, and Moore was the other—I did not look to see who I had got inside, but when I set them down I saw it was Walker and Ellard—when I turned to drive away I saw a policeman, and he asked the number of ray badge—I had a conversation with him, and he went to the house which the prisoners had gone into.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What time was this? A. Between two and three o'clock—I never heard anything against Amer.
Cross-examined by MS. BALDWIN. Q. You say Walker was one who got out of the cab? A. Yes—I had never seen him before—I saw him again at the station—I believe I said then that he was the man—I cannot swear that I did—Walker asked me if he was the man, and I told him I believed him to be one—I told him I was pretty well sure he was the same party—I had no doubt, but I was confused—he is the man that got out of the cab—I had known Ellard before by his using the same watering-house—it was not very dark, there was a gas just by—I did not look very particularly at him—I do not know anything particular about his person.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Did Ellard stagger out of the cab? A. I cannot say that he did—I believe they had been drinking.
Moore. Q. Will you swear I was in your cab that night? A. No—I swear you were outside—I swore so at the station.
GEORGE DOWNS ( police-constable F 101.) On the morning of the 28th of march, in conswquence of seeing the cab driven by Truk, I stopped him, and then I went to the second floor back room of a lodging-house—I saw the four prisoners standing round a bed in the room—Hannan, who keeps the house, was in the room—one of the prisoners, and to the best of my belief it was Moore, asked Hannan to advance 4 s. on the things, whick
were then strewed about the room—Hannan made some reply that he would not, or had not the money to lend them—I remained on the stairs for some time—Hannan left the room—I then heard a conversation with the prisoners about what they would do with the things, and they came to the conclusion that they would wack the swag —I then got four other constables, and gave the whole four prisoners into custody—when I got back to the room Walker had just walked into the adjoining room, and was taken there—this is the portmanteau I found in the room, and the property.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How could you see into the room? A. There was a candle in the room, and the door was not shut close—I was gone five or six minutes for the other constables—I had left a man at the door at the bottom—I am quite sure the persons who were in the room when I got back were the same who were there before—I stood peeping at them about five minutes.
Cross-examined by MR. BALDWIN. Q. Will you swear that Walker was there when you went up? A. Yes—I have known him for years—I heard bis voice, saying they would wack it, when they came to the conclusion that they must wack the swag —they were all in close conversation before I went to get assistance—it is a low lodging-house, where people pay 2 d. or 3 d. for a bed—there was nobody in that room but the prisoners and the landlord—there were five other persons in the room that Walker went into, where he was apprehended.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. You had seen the landlord in the room before you went for your brother constables? A. Yes—he came out of the room—I was standing in a recess on the stairs—he brushed against me, and he said, "Who is here?"—I said, "It is I, Hannan"—(be knew me)—Ellard appeared to be sober.
Moore. Q. Will you swear that I asked Hannan to lend 4 s. on the property? A. Yes, it was your voice—you were directing your attention to him at the time.
JAMES HANNAN . I live at No. 28, Sbort's-gtrdens, Drury-lane. Moore has lodged with me, off and on, for six months. On the morning of the 28th of March, he asked me for the key—I got out of bed, gave him the candle and the key, and he went up to his room, I suppose—I went up to that room in about four minutes afterwards, and the policeman was behind me—I saw three young men in that room, whom I had never seen before—I did not take proper notice of them, and cannot swear to them—the policemen took those four men—I did not see anything that was inside the room—Moore asked me to lend him 4 s. till the morning—I said I had not such a thing, which I had not.
Moore. Q. Did I ask you to lend me 4 s. on any property? A. No.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Were you in the room before you saw the prisoners taken? A. No—I went up stairs, and the policeman tapped me on the shoulder, and said, "Don't speak a word," and I did not—I stood still for very near ten minutes.
Moore's Defence. I went home to go to bed; I had not been in half-anhour when the policeman knocked; I opened the door, and in be walked, and four other policemen, and he pulled a portmanteau from under the bed; he said, "It is all right!"—I said, "I don't know what you mean."
GEORGE FOSTER ( City police-constable, No. 425.) I produce a certificate of Walker's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read Convicted
16 th Sept., 1844, and imprisoned six months)—the prisoners is the person.
(Ellard received a good character.)
ELLARD — GUILTY . Aged 25.
AMER — GUILTY . Aged 24.
MOORE — GUILTY . Aged 33.
Confined Six Months
WALKER — GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.

965. JOHN IRVINA was indicted for stealing 1 ladder, value 5 s., the goods of Henry William Dring, in a vessel, in a port of entry and discharge.
CHARLES JAMES ARNOLD . I am ship-keeper of the schooner Bailard, which was lying in the East and West India Docks. On the 22nd of March I lost a three-step ladder, which I had seen safe on the deck at three o'clock that day—I asked the prisoner who was mate of the Providence, which was lying alongside of the schooner, if he had got my ladder—he said, no—on the Thursday following I got an officer, aud went with him on board the Providence—I saw the ladder found on board the Providence aft the pump-well—it belonged to Messrs. Foster and Smith, African merchants, and owners of the schooner—the captain's name if Henry William Dring.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had you the charge of the schooner? A. Yes—tbe captain was out of the vessel—a vessel called the Sea Witch was lying astern of the Providence —have heard that the persons on board that vessel bad stolen the ladder belonging to the Providence—tbe prisoner said that as they had taken his ladder, he had taken mine—he would derive no advantage from the ladder, it was for the use of the ship.
NOT GUILTY .

968. WILLIAM HENRY WHITE was indicted for stealing 3 bottles, value 3 d.; 1 pint of wine, 2 s.; 1 pint of ale, 4 d.; 1 quart of stout, 8 d.; 3 half-crowns, 13 shillings, and 7 sixpences; the property of Gustavus Dunning, his master.—Two other COUNTS, stating it to be the property of the Sheriff of Middlesex.—And another, stating it to be the property of Laing Johnson.
MR. O'BRIEN conducted the Prosecution.
LAING JOHNSON . I was a wine-merchant, carrying on business at Hammersmith. I had an execution put into my house on Wednesday, the 19th of Feb.—the prisoner was put in possession of my premises by the Sheriff's officer that evening—I found him there the next morning—on the evening he was put in possession there was 17 s. 6 d. in the till—I examined the till on the Sunday, and there was nothing in it—I did not give him authority to remove wine, or ale, or beer.
JAMES JENNINGS . I was in the service of Mr. Dunning. The prisoner was in possession of his premises—on Thursday, the 20th of Feb., I recollect seeing a woman there who had come to him—the prisoner asked me for a light, and went down into the cellar—I saw him take a bottle of ale, a bottle of wine, and a battle of stout, and give them to her.
GUSTAVUS DUNNING . I put the prisoner in possession of the premises on the 19th of Feb.—he was to take the money that was received—I did not give him authority to remove anything.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. It would be his duty to render you an account at the end of the time, of money received, and goods he might happen to sell? A. Yes—he would have to sell, and to account to me for the produce—there was 1 l. 5 s. due to him—if he had entered the ale and wine against his account it would have been allowed in that manner—I believe he has had no opportunity of rendering his account—I have a little book of his, in which these articles are entered—I do not know when the entries were made.
GEORGE LOW ( police-constable T 50.) I took the prisoner—I found 1 1/2 d. on him.
NOT GUILTY .

969. JOHN NASH was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 3 l.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 18 s.; 1/4 of a yard of tweed, 4 s.; 3/ 8th of a yard of woollen cloth, 4 s.; 1 half-sovereign, 1 crown, 2 half-crowns, and 3 shillings; the property of William McDonough, his master.
WILLIAM MCDONOUGH . I am a tailor, and live in Gloucester-street, Queen-square—I have a shop in Henrietta-street, Covent-garden. The prisoner was in my employ for six or seven weeks previous to the 22nd of March—it was his duty to work as journeyman in my shop, and take orders there—he slept there—on Saturday night, the 22nd of March, I saw him at the shop, at half-past seven o'clock—I went again on Monday morning, the 24th, between nine and ten o'clock, and found it closed—no one had the care of it but the prisoner—I got in by means of a key which opens the back door in the passage—I found the gas was alight in the shop—I missed 16 s. from the desk, a coat, a pair of trowsers, two pairs of
shears, and two waistcoat pieces—I had seen them all safe on Saturday night, the 22nd—one of the pairs of shears, and this pair of trowsers, were on the prisoner's person when he was taken—I gave them him to make—he finished them on the 22nd, and was to deliver them to a gentleman that night—he came to my house at nine o'clock on the Saturday night, and I made him a present of a second-hand pair of trowsers of my son's—I did not see him again till he was in custody.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. You say he was to have taken them home to a gentleman that evening? A. Yes—they are completely finished—I saw him in custody between three and four o'clock on the day I went to the shop—these trowsers, which he was to have taken home on the Saturday night, were then on him—they are worth 18 s.—I have not found any of the other missing articles—when the prisoner was at my house at nine o'clock that night he bad a pair of new boots, but he had not these trowsers—I expected him to have taken the trowsers home before he came to me—he did not take them home, and the gentleman refused to take them in—the prisoner never offered them to him.
COURT. Q. How do you know that? A. Because I was obliged to make the gentleman another pair instead of them—I do not know that he did not give them to the prisoner—Richard Harvey occasionally worked for me—I owed him 7 s.—I gave the prisoner a 5 s. piece, and a half-crown to pay to Harvey, about six o'clock that evening.
RICHARD HARVEY . I live in Drory-lane. I am sometimes employed by the prosecutor—previous to the 22nd of March he owed me 7 s., the balance for making a coat—I went on that Saturday evening to bis shop for the money, about eight o'clock—I found the shop closed—I have not received any of the money.
HENRY SMITH ( police-constable A 28.) I apprehended the prisoner at a beer-shop in Cromer-street, on Monday, the 24th of March, about half-past three o'clock—I told him it was for absconding with a coat and trowsers, and some money—he did not say anything—he was drank—these trowsers were on him.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.

970. DEBORAH RAVEN was indicted for stealing 11 plates, value 1 l. 2 s.; 6 cups, 10 s.; 6 saucers, 10 s.; 2 wine-glasses, 1 s.; 2 egg-cups, 4 d.; 1 ale-glass, 3 d.; 2 rings, 13 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, 3 s.; 1 pair of cuffs, 6 d.; 1 shirt, 3 d.; 2 napkins, 1 s. 6 d.; 3 brooches, 3 s.; 1 pair of ear-drops, 6 d.; 1 purse, 5 s.; 1 snap, 5 s.; 2 cups, 6 d.; and 1 seal, 3 d.; the goods of Thomas Price Jones, her master.
THOMAS PRICE JONES . I live in Tothill-street, Westminster, and am a pawnbroker. In consequence of information, I went to No. 22, New Peter-street, on the 14th of March—I found a box there—the policeman who was with me opened it—I found in it half a dozen cups and saucers, some plates and glasses, and egg-cups—I knew them to be mine—they have been in our family for fifty years—I then went with the officer to No. 3, Great Titchfield-street—I saw the prisoner there—I saw her searched, and a ring and the duplicate of a purse were found on her—the purse belongs to my daughter—the ring is mine—the policeman searched a box there, which the prisoner said was hers, and in it he found these three brooches, these ear-drops, and other articles, which are mine—the prisoner had been in
my service, and left the 5th of March—I do not know when these articles were on my premises—I did not miss them.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Were not some of them found in the box of a person named Mansfield? A. Yes, the cups and saucers, and some other things—I do not know Mansfield, but he was known to some person in my house, as a kind of sweetheart of the prisoner—these articles of jewellery were kept in my wife's drawer—this purse was taken from my little girl's pocket.
WILLIAM MILLERMAN ( police-constable B 95.) I went with Mr. Jones to Great Titchfield-street, and found the prisoner—she was stooping down and putting her boot on, she had a duplicate and a gold ring in her hand—I went with the duplicate, and got this purse from a pawnbroker's—Mr. Jones left the prisoner with me, and she begged me not to show him the duplicate and the ring—I found these other articles at Peter-street.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was Mansfield lodging? A. At Peter-street—some of these things were found there.
JOHN HEALEY . I am shopman to Mr. Jones—this ring, which was found at Mansfield's, is Mr. Jones's property.
CECILIA COSGROVE . I live at No. 19, New Peter-street, Westminster. On the 14th of March the prisoner gave me this scarf, this plate, and this little snap—she came to me on the 5th of March, and left a box—she came on the 13th, and slept there—Mansfield used to come to see her—I taw her give him fifteen plates, two egg-cops, two wine-glasses, a ring, and half a dozen cups and saucers—she told him she bought them at a sale—these are them.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe she said something about being a corpse to-morrow? A. Yes—she said, "Take these, in remembrance of me; I bought them at a sale, and the ring I had from my mother's death, and you will hear to-morrow that I shall be a corpse"—she had got a bottle and some laudanum.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 20.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Four Months.

971. JAMES GORING was indicted for embezzlement.
RICHARD BADRINO . I am a cow-keeper, and live in Old Boswell-court—the prisoner was in my service, and had to go round with milk, and to receive money—I enter in a book an account of what he does not receive, and when he received money, either I or my wife took the account of him, and marked it off in the book—I supply Mrs. Duncan with milk—I did not receive any money on her account, from the 17th of March to the 23rd—I have the book here—I have entries of milk to Duncan, on the 22nd and 23rd of March, but I did not receive any money on either of those days.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Is this book your writing? A. My wife made some of the entries—these entries of 22nd and 23rd of March are my writing—my wife marks the book off as well as me—she took the account more frequently than I did, and she is not here.
NOT GUILTY .

972. THOMAS WILLIAMSON was indicted for stealing 11lbs., weight of lead, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of William Dent and another, his masters.

another—they are plumbers—the prisoner was in their employ—on the morning of the 4th of April, I stopped the prisoner as he was leaving the warehouse—I called him into the counting-house, and told him I suspected he had got some lead on his person—he put his hand into his pocket, and pulled out 11lbs. of lead—he said he hoped Mr. Dent would forgive him, it was his first offence—this is the lead—it is my masters'.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. What was the prisoner work ing at? A. Cutting lead into small pieces to stow it tway—it comes to us in large pieces—he was going to breakfast when he was charged with this—he said at once he had a piece of lead—he had worked for my master about two years, and up to this time we had every confidence in him—he said he had broken his knife in cutting the lead, and took this lead to repay him.

974. SARAH LIMB was indicted for stealing 12 sovereigns, 12 half-sovereigns, 1 crown, 16 half-crowns, 11 shillings, 8 sixpences, And 3 groats, the monies of James Nunn.
( The prosecutor being deaf and dumb gave his evidence by signs, which were interpreted.)
JAMES NUNN . I lodged and slept in a back room up stairs at the prisoner's house—no one slept in the room but me—on the morning of the 4th of April, I went out at six o'clock—I left a box locked up in my room, in which was 18 l. in gold, And 3 l. in silver—there were sovereigns, half-sovereigns, a crown, some half-crowns, shillings, and other silver, and some clothes—I know the money was all right the night before, and the box was locked—I locked it every night and morning—I returned home at seven o'clock in the evening—I could see that my box had been broken open, and the things inside had been all upset—I made the prisoner understand
by writing on a slate that my box had been broken open, and the money was all gone—she went up stairs with me, and I showed her my box—she had never seen the money in my box in my presence, but her daughter had—I made signs to the prisoner that I would get a policeman—my money had been in a bag—this is the bag it was in.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. How long had you been lodging with the prisoner? A. Two years and a half or three years—she was a well-conducted woman—her furniture was seized that day—I did not owe her any rent—I cannot tell how much there was in my bag when I received it back.
HENRY WOOD ( police-constable K 44.) On the evening of the 4th of April the prosecutor came to me at Poplar station—he showed me his shirt, which was torn—there was another person with him—I went to the prisoner's house in Grundy-street—I found the door partly open, and the prisoner was in the passage—she went iuto a back room—I followed her—she took hold of the prosecutor, and said, " Jem, don't give me in charge, I took your money, and will pay you back again"—she put this bag into his hand, containing six half-sovereigns, one crown, and some other silver—I went up stairs, and found the box with the mark of a chisel near the lock.
Cross-examined. Q. What was the amount of the money in the bag? A. 4 l. 16 s. 10 d.—she gave me a little silver at the station.
CHARLES DAY . I am a broker. I went to the prisoner's house to levy for arrears of rent on the 4th of April—the amount was 14 l. 1 s. 8 d.—she said she could not pay me—I said I was sorry to come to see her again, as I had distrained on her fourteen months before—I proceeded to take the inventory—she said she would get the money—she brought 15 l. in sovereigns and half-sovereigns—she got change, and paid me.
NOT GUILTY .

977. CHARLES WAGSTAFF was again indicted for assaulting Thomas Balcher, a police-officer, in the execution of his duty.—2nd COUNT, for a common assault.
THOMAS BALCHER ( City police-constable, No. 618.) I was called on on the morning of the 26th of March to take the prisoner—I asked him who and what he was—he said he did not know—I asked him to give some account of himself—he hesitated, and I said if he did not give an account he must go to the station—I took hold of his collar, and he turned and struck me a violent blow on the mouth, and knocked out two of my teeth.
Prisoner. Q. Did you produce the teeth before the Lord Mayor? A. I swallowed one, I could not produce that—I produced one piece of a tooth—I was under the doctor's hands for six days—I drank one glass of ale next day with your master.
JURY. Q. Was the prisoner tipsy? A. Not when I took him.
SUSANNAH POOL . I live at Mr. Leach's. I sent for the policeman, and gave the prisoner in charge—I saw him strike the policeman, and then the policeman struck him with his staff—the prisoner struck first.
( Witnesses for the Defence.)
HARRIET LEACH . I was present on this occasion—when the policeman was sent for the prisoner stood in the passage, and a scuffle ensued—I did not see who struck the first blow, but I saw the prisoner's elbow catch the policeman in the mouth—the policeman struck him four or five times.
CHARLES LEACH . I am ten years old—I was present—the prisoner and the policeman were struggling, and the prisoner's elbow hit the policeman in the mouth, and then the policeman held him down and struck him four or five times with his staff.
JAMES LEACH . I keep the house where the prisoner was taken—I can swear on my oath that the policeman has not lost a tooth now, for two. years ago he called on me, and I saw he wanted two teeth then.
COURT to THOMAS BALCHER. Q. On your oath, do you swear that you lost two teeth in consequence of the blow the prisoner gave you? do, my Lord.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— confined One Month.
