978. JAMES CORNISH and WILLIAM CANNON were indicted for feloniously breaking and entering a building, within the curtilage of the dwelling-house of George Cossar, on the 9th of March, at Walthamstow, and stealing therein, 1 basket, value 2 s.; 15 chisels, 15 s.; 11 guages, 11 s.; 12 screw-drivers, 12 s.; 2 gouges, 2 s.; 19 brad-awls, 3 s.; 4 saws, 2 l.; 30 planes, 5 l.; 7 plough irons, 9 s.; and great variety of other tools, his property.—2nd COUNT, calling it a shop.
CANNON pleaded GUILTY, and received a good character.— Rrecommended to mercy. Aged 21.— Confined Two Years.
GEORGE COSSAR . I am a carpenter, and live at Walthamstow. On Sunday, the 9th of March, I saw my tools safe in my workshop, which ii within a few yards of my dwelling-house—the dwelling-house and workshop are all inclosed by a pale fence—both have doors opening into tne yard—I missed the tools on Monday morning at seven o'clock—the door of the workshop had been forced open, apparently with a chisel—I saw my tools again on the Saturday following at we Police-court.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. I suppose you had not ustd your tools on Sunday? A. No-they were locked up in my chest, but I looked into the chest—I found the chest broken open.
THOMAS WILLIAM ELVIDGE . I am in the service of Worrall and Pownall, pawnbrokers. I produce a smoothing-plane, twtnty-six chisels, and fifty-five tools in a basket, which were pawned with roe on Monday, tae 10th, I think—I also produce two planes pawned the name of charles Jones—I do not know by whom.
JOHN MINOAY . A chisel was brought to me by the policeman—I know Cannon, and am quite certain he had that chisel at the time he worked for me.
WILLIAM NICHOLL . I was a policeman at this time, and took the prisoners into custody on Wednesday night, the 12th of March, about nine o'clock—each had a bundle—I went up and said to Cannon, "What have you got here?"—he said, "Tools"—I said, "What tools?"—he said, "Tools I use in my work"—he said, "I have been at work at the docks"—I said, "At the docks at this time of night?"—he said, "If you will go to my master, Mr. Mingay, he will tell you it is right"—I took them to the station—I found six planes in Cannon's bundle—I did not search Cornish's bundle—another constable stopped him, but at the station I observed something bulky in Cornish's pockets—I examined them, and found nine planes—he said, "They are not mine, I am only carrying them for Cannon."
AUGUSTUS BRICE ( policeman.) I was at the station-house when the bundle was taken from Cannon—I observed he was bulky, and asked what he had got there—he said, "Feel and see"—I felt and found seven planes, seven plough-irons, and a broken chisel on him—he said he had brought them from the other side of Chelmsford—Cornish said he bad nothing to do with it, he was employed by Cannon to carry them.
CHARLES WOOD . I was with Nicholl at the time the prisoners were stopped—Cornish had this bundle in his hand—I asked what he had got there—he said they were tools—I asked him where he got them from—he said he had been to fetch them out of pawn—I asked where—he said he was a coach-builder and was going into Rosemary-lane to work—the bundle contains five planes.
Cross-examined. Q. Who was present when he said this about Rosemary-lane 1 A. Nobody; he said so in Commercial-road—the other two officers were nine or ten yards before me with the other prisoner—I did not hear what Cornish said at the station.
FREDERICK COSSAR . I am the prosecutor's son. Cornish has worked with me some months ago—I have known Cannon work at a wheelwright's shop close by—I have seen them together on the forest close by, several times—Cornish has not worked in our workshop—he worked as a sawyer—he has been in the shop but not with any of the tools—I know these planes found in Cornish's bundle—they have my name on them—I know all the tools in this bundle—these found in Cornish's pocket have my father's name on them—they are all his, and part of the property missing—we never sell our tools—I have examined the rest of the property found on Cannon—they are all my father's.
CORNISH — GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Two Years.
Before Mr. Baron Platt.
[trialAccount t18450407-979]
979. The said JAMES CORNISH and WILLIAM HACK were indicted for stealing 2 sheep, value 4 l., the property of Charles Tebbutt.—2nd COUNT, for killing them with intent to steal the carcases.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES TEBBUTT . I live at Leytonstone—my house is on the high road from Stratford, to Woodford—at the back of the house is a meadow where I keep my sheep, and behind that is Hainault Forest—my garden wall abuts on a lane and on the high road, and my meadow on the forest—on the 9th of March I had five sheep in the meadow—in consequence of information I went there on the morning of the 12th, and there were only three sheep—I saw a gap in the fence which parts the meadow and field—in attempting to lift the sheep over, the wool had been torn—there
had been a slight frost, and I traced the footmarks of more than one person t short way—the footmarks were close to where the sheep had been lifted over the fence, and a little way along they were not plain enough to make any observation on them—Want and Prior afterwards showed me the carcases of two sheep.
GEORGE PRIOR . I am a gardener, in Mr. Tebbutt's employ. On the morning of 12th March I was at work in the garden—I went on the fofest with a policeman about twelve o'clock in the day, and saw him find the ear-case of one sheep about half a mile from Mr. Tebbutt's, on the forest—it was buried under the ground—I took part of the ground off it—on Friday, the 15th, I went with Want and brought that sheep home—we afterwards searched and found another place on the forest, and found another beep's carcase there, which we brought home—the entrails had been taken out and the throats cut—there was a great deal of blood—I do not know the marks on the sheep.
EDWARD WANT . On Tuesday night, the 11th of March, I saw Mr. Tebbutt's sheep safe at a quarter before six o'clock—I went into the meadow about half past nine o'clock on Wednesday morning, and missed two—I went on the Friday with Prior to the forest, and brought home the carcases of two sheep—they were the carcases of Mr. Tebbutt's sheep which I missed on the Wednesday—there was a long red mark from the neck nearly down to the tail, by which I know them—there were no letters on them—the skins are here—I took them off—the entrails had been taken oat before they were buried—the meat and skin were buried.
EDWIN DIVER ( policeman.) I was stationed at Leytonstone on the night of the 11th of March—I know the lane leading from the forest of Hainault to the high road, by the side of Mr. Tebbutt's house—it is called Davis-lane—I was in that lane about one o'clock in the morning, and saw the prisoner Hack there, close to Mr. Tebbutt's house, about 100 yards from the meadow—he was just moving—he appeared as if moving his feet—I asked why he was loitering about there—he gave me a saucy answer—I do not recollect his words—he after that said, "I am going to Woodford,"and proceeded on his way towards Woodford—I passed the place again, and at two o'clock saw him in company with Cornish—I was going towards the forest, and they were coming from it—I had my lantern—I believe they saw me, but they directly crossed the road, and quickly passed me—I turned my light on them as they passed—they walked quicker when they saw me—I walked after them, and then they ran—I am quite certain it was both the prisoners—I ran also, and overtook Cornish—Hack wa" forward—I asked Cornish what he was doing thew, and why he ran away—he did not give me a direct answer—I turned my light all round him, and saw his hands were covered with blood—I laid hold of him directly, and asked how he accounted for the blood on his hands—he gave me no direct answer, but struck me with his hand in the stomach, which took my wind away—I got hold of him—we scuffled together—he got away from me, and ran towards a fence of Mr. Davis's—I ran after him, but he leaped over the fence, which has spikes in it, and in my trying to get over, a spike ran into my hand—he got into the forest, and got away—I went on Wednesday to where the sheep was found—I watched on Wednesday night and Thursday, and nobody came for the sheep—on the Friday night I found Cornish in custody, and recognised him at once—I went with another officer in search of Hack, on the Monday following—I recognized him, and took him into custody—when Cornish was in custody
the front of his trowsers had blood on them, and also behind, and there was blood on his shirt sleeves and wristbands—I asked how he accounted for the blood—he said the front might be from his nose bleeding—I said, "That don't account for the blood behind"—he said he could not account for that, but he believed that was from the stains of ochre—they appeared to me stains of blood—they were down from the joint of the legs—the blood appeared in the same way as T have seen on butchers' dresses.
JOSEPH BENTON . I am a policeman of Leytonstone—I know the prisoners, and have seen them together more than once—I was on duty on the 10th and 11th of March—I did not see them together on either of those days.
Hack's Defence. I was in doors; the policeman came and looked all round, and went out again; then they took me out; I was about twenty yards from the house; he asked me if I was out that night, I said I could prove I was at home from eight o'clock at night till seven o'clock in the morning, by three men.
JAMES FAULKKNER . I lodge in the same room with Hack—I came home on the night of the 4th of March, between eight and nine o'clock, and he was then in bed—I went to bed and went to sleep—I got up about five o'clock, he was still in bed—I did not sleep in the same bed—a man slept with him who worked for Mr. Whale, the blacksmith, but he has left him a week or a fortnight back—I do not know his name—George Argent slept in the same bed as me—there were only us four sleeping in the room—I got up before Argent, and left him in bed—I left them all three in bed—Argent is a kind of odd man—he works close by—I do not know the name of the gentleman he works for, or his trade—I think his name is Thwaite—he has some business in town, and lives in a private house—he has a garden, and Argent works in his garden, and cleans knives and forks—I have not seen him do so, but he tells me he does—Hack and the blacksmith were in bed when I came borne that night—Argent came to bed after me—he was sitting by the fire taking his supper when I came in—I left him at his supper when I went to bed—I cannot tell exactly what his supper was—it might Jiave been bread and cheese, or bread and meat—I cannot tell—as soon as I go home I go to bed, and do not sit down ten minutes—he was not drinking anything that I know—he generally has a pipe after supper—I cannot say whether he had one that night—I went to sleep before he got to his pipe—I cannot say whether he had anything to drink while he was smoking that night—his pipe generally lies on the table, and did lie there that night—I am quite sure I went to sleep before be came to bed—I think this was on the 4th of March—I can hardly say what day of the week it was—it may be Wednesday or Tuesday—I think it was Wednesday—I am quite sure it was as early as the 4th of March—I made no memorandum of it, only having heard other people talking of it—I was not before the Magistrate—I heard on the Monday when I went home to dinner that Hack was taken that day for sheep stealing—it came to my mind that he was asleep in the same room with me on the 4th of March—I think it was the 4th of March.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Is this room where you slept on the ground floor? A. Yes—there is a window to the room opening into the yard—I have been attending here five days to speak for the prisoner—I came of my own accord—I heard he was going to be tried, from my sister-in-law—she is no relation to Hack—she lives in the same party like as we live—she had seen Hack that day—she did not tell me to say it was on the 4th of March that I had been with him.
Q. Who told you it was the 4th of March? A. I heard some young men talking about the 4th of March—they were not of our party—when they said it was 4th of March, I began to recollect all about the 4th of March—I did not recollect the day of the week—I recollected that it was the 4th of March when'the sheep were taken—I was not there when they were taken, only I heard them say about it on the Monday—I then recollected Hack was in the room with me—I am sure that was the day.
COURT. Q. What day of the month was Monday? A. I am no scholar, I cannot think of it now.
GEORGE ARGENT . I am a gentleman's senrant, and work in the garden of Mr. Drake, at Ley tonstone. I can take my oath that Hack was in bed on the night of the 4th of March—it was on the Tuesday—I heard of his being taken up on the Monday following—there were two beds in the room where we slept that night—I slept with John Faulkener—I was in bed first—he sat up by the fire taking his supper—I had just gone to bed before he came in—I do not recollect what he had for supper—he was in the other room—the living room is not the bed-room—he was sitting by the fire when I went to bed—Hack was in bed before I went to bed—Charles Baron slept with him—I cannot tell where Baron is—I was awake when Faulkener came to bed—I cannot tell whether he smoked his pipe before he came to bed—he is in the habit of smoking, and so was I after supper—I had my supper before he came in—I had some pickled pork—I left none for him—I did not finish it—we board ourselves. NOT GUILTY.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18450407-980]
980. ALFRED CALLOW and GEORGE MOUNTJOY were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of April, 1 mare, price 2 l., the property of Thomas Dyer.
THOMAS DYER . I am a carpenter, and live at Forest-gate, in Essex. I had a grey mare on the forest on the 5th of April—I saw her last at two o'clock that day—she is a very quiet mare—I went to look for her in the evening, finding she did not come home at her usual time, and at half-past ten I found her at the station—she was dose against Forest-gate, in West Ham parish, on the Wanstead-flats.
EDWIN DIVEB ( policeman.) I met the two prisoners about half-past seven o'clock in the evening, against Can Hall-gate, about a mile and a half from Wanstead-flats—it was not part of the forest—Callow was riding the mare—he had on a jacket and collar—Mountjoy was walking at the head of the mare—this is the bridle they had—( producing a boy's red comforter)—the toll-man stopped them, and demanded his fare, which was 1 1/2 d.—Mountjoy said, "Wont it do when we come back?"—I said to the toll-man, "I don't think this is right, I will question the boys"—I asked them who the mare belonged to—they said, "To Mr. Davis, an ironmonger, of Stratford"—knowing there was no such person in Stratford, I said, "Does he keep a shop!"—Callow said, "Yes, he keeps an ironmonger's shop, it has been lately opened"—I said, "How long has it been opened?"—at last Mountjoy said, "I will tell no more lies about it, we took the mare off the forest; I took this off my neck, and Callow put it on the horse's neck, and rode it away;" and they meant to take it to their uncle's stables, in Beer, or Vera-yard, I did not properly understand them at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. You were on duty at the toll-gate in uniform? A. Yes.
NOT GUILTY .
( The prisoners were only 12 years of age.)
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

981. JOHN RAINBIT was indicted for stealing 1 basket, value 1 s.; 3lbs. weight of butter, 2 s. 6 d.; and 3lbs. weight of sugar, 1 s. 9 d.; the goods of Samuel Rawlinson, from the person of Charles Rawlinson.
CHARLES RAWLINSON . I am twelve years old—my father's name is Samuel Rawlinson. On the 3rd of Jan. I had been to Romford to get some butter and sugar—I was in Burley-lane about five o'clock in the evening—the prisoner overtook me—he pushed me down, and took the basket from my hand, with the butter and sugar in it, and went away with it—he did not strike me—I had some money, but he did not take that—I saw him again on the Monday afterwards, against the Pigeons, at Stratford—he ran away—I saw him on the Wednesday after, at Stratford, and the policeman took him—I knew his person before, but I did not know his name.
WILLIAM RICHARDSON . I am an inspector of police. On the 3rd of Jan., about six o'clock in the evening, Charles Rawlinson came to me at Illford—he described the person to me, and said he should know him again—I sent an officer with him, but the prisoner was not found—on the Wednesday afterwards I saw him again in Stratford—I went to the King of Prussia—there were several persons there—he pointed out the prisoner, and I took him.
Prisoner's Defence. I was not at Barking from Nov. till the last day of Jan.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

[trialAccount t18450407-983]
983. WILLIAM SAVILLE and ROBERT LEWIS were indicted for stealing 60 yards of calico, value 1 l. 5 s.; and 1 wooden roller, 1 s.; the goods of Richard Turner Higgs; and that Saville had been before convicted of felony.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS JONES . I am in the employ of Richard Turner Higgs, a draper, at Stratford. On the 28th of Feb. I placed a roll of calico on the steps inside the lobby of the door—it was on a wooden roller, and I had put a mark on it—this is the roller—( looking at it)—I missed the calico the next morning, when Mr. Higgs told me of it—there were sixty yards of it.
JOSEPH STAINS . I am a painter. On the evening of the 28th of Feb. I was passing Mr. Higgs' shop—I observed the two prisoners standing together at the corner of his shop, and talking together, I believe, but I was not close enough to hear them—I passed a little way, then turned my head, and saw some person currying the roll of calico across the road under their arm.
Saville. Q. Why did you not immediately go to Mr. Higgs and tell him? A. I did not know it was Mr. Higgs's—I had noticed the prisoners standing there, but I did not notice any calico as I passed.
RACHEL RAINER . I deal in marine stores, and live at Stratford, opposite Mr. Higgs. On the 28th of Feb. the prisoners came to my shop between seven and eight o'clock in the evening, and asked me to buy a roll of calico—I would not have anything to do with it—they then asked me to lend them a bag to put it in—I declined—they threw the roll of calico into the yard, I believe, but I did not see it—they left the roller in our yard—I saw it that night—I did not see it brought in the next morning, but I saw it on the fire, and the policeman came and took it out of the fire.
WILLIAM POTTER ( police-constable K 273.) I went to Rainer's and found the roller in the fire—I took it out and showed it to Mr. Jones, who identified it—I had taken the prisoners before.
Lewis. When he took us he would not tell us what it was for; I did not know till I got to the station. Witness. I did tell them.
Saville. If it had been any other policeman they would have found the calico in Rainer's house; these policemen are drinking in her house when they ought to be on duty; she had been up here two or three times against boys for robbing.
COURT to R. RAINER. Q. Why did you not go to Mr. Higgs's? A. I was just putting my children to bed when I told the prisoners to take the calico away—I did not see it—it was on my premises a quarter of an hour—I could have gone to Mr. Higgs's—they had come and carried it off before my husband gave information.
JOSEPH BENTON ( police-constable K 181.) I prodoce a certificate of Saville's former conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office—( read—Convicted the Srd of Feb., 1845, and confined ten days)—he is the person.
SAVILLE — GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.
LEWIS — GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

985. DANIEL WASHBROOK and JAMES GURNEY were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of Edward Evans, from his person.
EDWARD EVANS . I live in Talbot-court, Gracechurch-street. On the 25th of March, about half-past three o'clock in the afternoon, I was at Greenwich, near the College-gate, looking at a man conjuring—I perceived nothing until the officer, who had both the prisoners in custody, showed me my handkerchief—this now produced is it—I know it was safe an hour before.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. There was a crowd round the conjuror? A. Yes, perhaps 100, or 150 people.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 108.) I was at Greenwich, and saw the prisoners by the College-gate—I followed them from the fair to the College—they were together—there was a crowd. I saw them go behind Evans, and saw Washbrook take the handkerchief from his pocket and hand it to Gurney—I secured them, and Glad win got the handkerchief.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( policeman.) I saw the prisoners by the prosecutor—White took Washbrook, I took Gurney, and took this handkerchief out of his pocket.
Gurney's Defence. Washbrook told me he had picked up the handkerchief; he gave it to me and went away.
(The prisoners received good characters.)
WASHBROOK — GUILTY . Aged 13.
GURNEY — GUILTY . Aged 16.
Confined Two Months.

987. ELIZABETH GEORGE was indicted for unlawfully obtaining, on the 22nd of Nov., half a sovereign, and other monies, of Charlotte Melhuish, her mistress, by false pretences.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450407-988]
988. ELIZABETH GEORGE was again indicted for unlawfully obtaining, on the 8th of March, 1 half-sovereign, I half-crown, 2 shillings, 1 sixpence, and 4 pence; the monies of Charlotte Melhuish, her mistress, by false pretences.
MR. CROUCH conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLOTTE MELHUISH . I am a widow, and live in Park-grove, Black-heath. The prisoner entered my service last Aug.—I was in the habit of giving her money every week to pay the tradespeople—on the 8th of March I paid her 15 s. 4 s.—she gave me an account at that time, of what money she had paid, which I wrote down—among other things, here is, "Meat, 5 s. 8 d.; and beer, 2 s. 3 d."—that was written down by her dictation at the time; and, in consequence of her statement, I gave her half a sovereign
and some shillings, making 15 s. 4 d., the amount she stated—I give her that money, believing she had paid the tradespeople for all those articles—Mr. Williams is my butcher, and Mr. Evans the publican—I desired her to deal with Evans.
HARRIET WILLIAMS . I am the daughter of John Williams, a butcher, in Spencer's-place, Blackheath—we supply the progenitrix with meat—the prisoner was in the habit of coming for it—I supplied her with meat the week before the 8th of March—there was 4 s. 9 d., due for that week, which has never been paid.
Prisoner. I paid you 10 s. for a piece of beef and a leg of mutton that week. Witness. She paid for two joints that week, which came to 10 s. 3 1/2 d.—that was besides the 4 s. 9 d. that has not been paid—I did not deliver her meat to the amount of 4 s. 9 d. that week—I have a memorandum, which I made out the other day for the purpose of coming here—I did not deliver her meat to the amount of 4 s. 9 d. the week before the 8th of March—I cannot recollect whit she had that week—I know she had two joints, and those she paid for.
SARAH EVANS . I supplied the prosecutrix with beer, which my boy delivered to the prisoner at Mrs. Melhuish's house—4 s. 4 d. was due for the week before the 9th of March—I asked the prisoner that day to ask Mrs. Melhuish to pay the bills, which amounted to 2 l. 10 s. 2 d., for beer delivered—she said Mrs. Melhuish was coming he me in a day or two, and she would pay it—she did not pay me 2 s. 3 d. that week—she paid me two sovereigns on the 16th of March
Prisoner. And I told you I would pay the other the moment I got it.
JOHN WATERS ( police-constable R 177.) I took the prisoner into custody—on going to the station she said she hoped I would call on Mr. Millington and Mr. Williams, and beg them to be her friends.
Prisoner's Defence. I never had more than half-a-sovereign, with which I paid the butcher's bill for a piece of veal and some beef; mistress had the bill and receipt; she owed me 11 s., and I never bad it back again; she only allowed me 4 s. a week to keep me, and, my health being bad, I was obliged to have a charwoman, and pay her; mistress was only at home two or three days a week, and the house was left solely to me, with lodgers; I had not the slightest idea of wronging any one of a halfpenny, and I should have paid all up if they had allowed me time.
MRS. MILHUISH re-examined.
I am sure that out of the 15 s. 4 d. which I paid her on the 8th of March 2 s., 3 d. was for beer.
GUILTY of obtaining the 2 s. 3 d. Aged 38.— Confined Six Months.
(Mrs. Melhuish stated the amount altogether obtained by the prisoner to be 16 l. or 17 l.)
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18450407-989]
989. WILLIAM HARRISON was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of March, 1 watch, value 5 l. 10 s., the goods of Samuel Pearson, in a vessel on the navigable river Thames; and JOHN CORMACK , for feloniously receiving the same; well knowing it to have been stolen; to which
HARRISON pleaded GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Six Months.
SAMUEL PEARSON . I am mate of the brig Union. On Thursday, the 20th of March, she was lying in Bugsby's-hole, below Blackwall—Harrison was an apprentice belonging to the ship—about ten minutes past four o'clock, on the 19th of March, I came ashore, leaving him in charge of the
cabin—I returned about five, and he was pone—I saw him no more till he was in custody—I directly missed my watch, which 1 had left hanging on the starboard side of the cabin—I have never seen it since.
GEORGE SATCHELL . I am a seaman on board the Union. On the Thursday morning after this I met Harrison in Church-lane, and gave him in charge.
JOHN BURKE ( police-constable H 81.) Harrison was given into my charge—he offered at the station-house to show me who he took the watch to if I took him to Cooper's-row—I did so, and Cormack was then apprehended.
JOHN CONDON ( policeman.) I went with Harrison to No. 2, Cooper's-alley, and saw Cormack sitting in the house—Harrison said, "That is the man I sold the watch to"—Cormack said he knew nothing of the watch or Harrison—the boy Norton was there, and said, "It is no use denying it; I saw him buy the watch at the straw yard; he gave 5 s. for it."
JOSEPH NORTON . I have been sleeping in the straw-yard in Ratcliff-highway—I know Cormack—he slept in the same place—I saw Harrison and him together on a Wednesday evening, just by the fire-place, and heard Harrison say to Cormack that he had a watch to sell him—Cormack told him to keep it till the morning, and he would look at it—Harrison asked him 10 s. for it—Harrison slept in the straw-yard that night—next morning they came into the room where they sell coffee—I then saw the watch, and took it in my hand—it was silver, with a little steel chain, and a seal, marked, "One penny"—I saw Harrison sell it to Cormack for 5 s.—I saw the money pass—Cormack borrowed it from a man.
Cormack. Q. When did you see me with Harrison? A. On Wednesday night, just after they all went in—you borrowed the money of Joe Collier, who belongs to the Marine Society, and you paid him the money out of a sovereign.
S. PEARSON re-examined. There was a steel chain to my watch, a key, and a seal with "One Penny" marked on the lower part of it.
CORMACK — GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

993. HANNAH MCCARTHY was indicted for stealing 1 pair of boots, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of John Slater.
ANTHONY TOOK . I am a shoemaker, and live at Deptford. These boots are John Slater's—I had them safe in my shop on the morning of
the 10th of March—the prisoner came that day for a pair of children's boots—she examined them outside the shop, brought them in, and asked the price—she left them on the counter, and went out—she was not out long before she passed the window again, took these boots, and ran away—I followed her—she was taken afterwards.
Prisoner. Q. Why not stop me? A. I had no one to mind my shop, or you would not have got from me—I saw you again on the Saturday night, and I made my boy pursue you to see where you went—I have no mark on these boots—I know I lost a pair like these.
JAMES HASTIE ( policeman.) I went to the prisoner's lodging, and asked where the boots were—she said, "Here they are; I did not steal them"—in going to the station she said she bought them of a woman who travelled with a basket, but did not know where she lived—at the station she told the man she would give him the boots, and pay for the damage done to them—she said she had bought them on the Friday before the robbery.
Prisoner's Defence. My child had these boots to wear; I bought them of a poor widow, ten or eleven days before the prosecutor lost his.
THOMAS CAIRN . I am the prisoner's landlord. She brought down these boots to me on Sunday, the 2nd of March, and put them on the child's feet.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450407-994]
994. GEORGE WILLIAMS and EDWARD GILLETT were indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 6 d.; 1 sixpence, 1 penny, and 7 halfpence; the property of Rebecca Hart, from her person; to which
WILLIAMS pleaded GUILTY . Aged 14.— Confined Three Months.
REBECCA HART . I am single, and live in Giffin-street, Deptford. On the 24th of March, about six o'clock in the evening, I was in Greenwich fair—a policeman asked if I had lost my handkerchief, and I found I had—it had contained 10 1/2 d.—this is it.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( police-constabit R 122.) I was at the fair—I saw the two prisoners together for half an hour—I saw them both attempt gentlemen's pockets, by putting their hands in, and feeling their pockets—I afterwards saw Williams put his hand into the prosecutrix's pocket, and take out this handkerchief—he was passing it to Gillett—I seized him, and it fell on the ground—I took both the prisoners, and took up the handkerchief, and found this money in it.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Was the fair crowded? A. It was—I was watching the prisoners—I saw them attempt to pick pockets, but not pick one before this—the people were not pushing about so much as they had been at the time this was taken—the prosecutrix was standing looking at a show.
(Gillett received a good character.)
GILLETT — GUILTY . Aged 16.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18450407-995]
995. DANIEL CUMMINGS and THOMAS RUSSELL were indicted for stealing, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of James Barber, from his person.
JAMKS BARBER . I am a printer. I went to Greenwich fair on the the 25th of March—an officer spoke to me, and I missed my handkerchief—he took the prisoners into a shop, and found my handkerchief on Russell—this is it.
JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) I was at the fair, looking after thieves—I saw the two prisoners in company go behind the prosecutor—I had seen them together about half an hour—they stood by the side of each other—Russell put his right hand into the prosecutor's left-hand pocket, and took out his handkerchief—I took the prisoners, and between Russell's shirt and his skin I found the handkerchief. GEORGE MARTIN ( police-constable R 93.) I was at the fair about two hours before this—I saw the prisoners together three or four different times—Russell attempted several pockets, and Cummings was close behind him—I took Cuminings, and found on him a pair of scissors and a tobacco-box.
CUMMINGS — NOT GUILTY .
RUSSELL — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18450407-996]
996. HENRY CAVANNAGH was indicted for stealing 1 sovereign, 1 half-crown, 1 sixpence, and 2 pence, the monies of David Charles Smith, from the person of Elizabeth Smith.
ELIZABETH SMITH . I am the wife of David Charles Smith—we live in Poplar-place, Lewisham—on the evening of the 17th of March I was in the gallery of Deptford Theatre, with my neighbours—I had a sovereign, a half-crown, a sixpence, and some coppers in my pocket—I held my pocket while I was in the gallery—I did not sit—I was standing—I felt a person crowd against me, and felt a sensation at my neck, which induced me to let go of my pocket—I did not miss my money till a quarter to ten, as I was going to leave the theatre, Mrs. Reed then said something to me, and then I missed my money—I saw the prisoner in the gallery—I came down and heard a scuffle—the prisoner passed me in going out—he made use of abusive language, and said, "You have lost nothing"—he was searched, and a half-crown, a sixpence, and two pence were found on him—there was no sovereign found, but he was seen to swallow something.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. I suppose the gallery was crowded where you were standing? A. No, it was not—there were a number of persons standing as well as I was—I did not go out to take any refreshment, nor take any next door before I went in.
ALICE REED . I was with Mrs. Smith. I saw the prisoner close behind me—I saw my gown pulled up, and I missed 3 s.—no one was with me but Mrs. Smith, and Mrs. Fell—we were standing—the prisoner was behind us very close, and I saw another man with him up in the gallery.
Cross-examined. Q. Was that other man near you? A. No, not that I saw—there were two or three other men—Mrs. Smith and I stood the whole time—we did not sit down, because we meant to leave.
MARY FELL . I went to the theatre with this party. I saw the prisoner standing behind us the whole evening—the policeman took him—I saw him swallow something.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you see him chewing anything before he swallowed anything? A. No—I do not know whether there is a staircase leading from the gallery to the public-house next door.
JOHN STEVENS . I was present when the prisoner was searched—I saw something pass from one side of his mouth to the other—I said, "He has something in his mouth"—he appeared to swallow it.
Cross-examined. Q. Had he a handkerchief round his neck A. I did not notice—his neck was not bare—there is a passage down from the gallery to the public-house.
GEORGE TELFORD ( police-constable R 185.) I took the prisoner—I found on him a half-crown, a sixpence, one penny, and two halfpence—one of the halfpence is marked—the prosecutrix identified it—I saw the prisoner swallow something.
ELIZABETH SMITH re-examined. I had a halfpenny that was peculiar—this is it—it is a harp halfpenny, and has a hole near the edge—the money found on the prisoner exactly corresponds with mine except the sovereign.
Cross-examined. Q. How lately before had you had that halfpenny? A. I took it at twelve o'clock that day—I did not spend any halfpence after that, nor take any change.
GUILTY .† Aged 23.— Transported for Ten Years.

SAMUEL GREGORY . I am a surgeon, and live at Greenwich. Prisoner. He asked me to go with him to the Bee Hive, and we had three glasses of brandy and water; I felt myself getting giddy, and said I would go home to my father; he then took me to this house of ill-fame; he said he should like to have half an hour's sleep; he gave me the money, and I went out of the room; the woman said, "Are you going, what are you going to stand?" I gave her half-a-crown.
JOHN WHEEBLE ( police-constable R 300.) I met the prosecutor about half-past one o'clock—I found the prisoner within fifty or sixty yards of her father's house—before I could say anything she ran away—I took her—she said, "I own I have robbed the gentleman, and I have the whole of the property in my purse, and the watch-key likewise"—I said, "Give it to me," and she did—she said, "I have only spent the money lie gave me to go with him;" and she told the inspector so.
Prisoner. No, he said, "You have robbed this gentleman;" I said, "I own I have it, but he gave it me."
NOT GUILTY .

DAVIS pleaded GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Three Months.

JOHN WHITE ( police-constable R 180.) On the 29th of March, I saw the four prisoners in High-street, Deptford—I watched them for an how, and about seven o'clock I saw the whole four of them go and stand within about three yards of Mr. Watts's shop—I then saw Jones and Doughty
cross the road, leaving the other two close to Mr. Watts's shop—in about five minutes I saw Davis coming from the shop, in company with M'Carthy, with the pair of trowsers—they crossed over to the other two, and they all walked away—as soon as they got away we took them—Davis had these trowsers—I took Doughty and Jones, and before I had said what I wanted them for, Doughty said, "We were not in company with the other two."
JOHN EVANS ( police-constable R 190.) Jones's Defence. I was coming from Blackheath; I met this young man; we were going down the road, and the officer came and took hold of me and Doughty, and said it was for a pair of trowsers.
Doughty's Defence. My father sent me down to look for a ship; I came back, and met this lad; he asked me where I was going, I said, "To the highway, to cross the water."

ROBERT SEDDON . I am a carpenter, and live at the railway station, New Cross, Deptford. In the middle of the afternoon, on the 31st of March, I was buying oranges near the Montague Arms at Deptford—the prisoner came up and asked if I would stand anything—we went to the Montague Arms, and were under a shed outside—I had three halt-sovereigns and other money in my pocket—she then took me to a room, where I suppose she lived, and sat down by the side of me—I then felt something come from
my trowsers pocket, and she stepped out of the door—I stood up, felt in my pocket, and my money was gone—I went out, but could not see the prisoner—I staid there till Mrs. Blogden came and asked me if I had had some money taken from me—she said the person had run round the corner.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. What time was this? A. About five in the afternoon—I walked half mile with her I dare say—we did not stop at one or two places before we got to the Montague Arms—I was sober—there was another woman with her at the Montague Arms—it was in her room I felt the money drawn from my pocket—there was no other woman with me in the room at the time my money was missing—I was not on the bed, but sitting on a chair—she did not gift me time to stop her hand in coming from my pocket—my money was safe not more than three quarters of an hour before; I had counted it over in coming along, and separated the gold from the silver in the street—the gold was in my waistcoat pocket and the silver in my trowsers—I felt nothing drawn from my waistcoat pocket—all my money was missing—I went down in the house for about five minutes to have something to eat, but that was before I missed the money—I did not meet any other girls below.
ELIZABETH HARWOOD . On the 31st of March the prisoner came by our door, and shook something in her hand like money, but I did not see It—she said to Mrs. Blogden, "You b—y w—"—she then turned to another person, and said, "I have picked him up, and we must cut."
Cross-examined. Q. Where were you? A. At my own door.
CHARLOTTE BLOGDEN . I saw the prisoner come up the street—she made a full stop, and shook something like money—she said to me, "You b—y w—," and then she turned to Brown, and said, "I have picked the b—up, and we must cut; I have got his rowdey. "
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined One Year.

1003. JOHN CLARKE and HENRY HURRY were indicted for stealing 33 panes of glass, value 1 l. 10 s.; and 7 glazed window-sashes, 5 l.; the goods of William Seaborn, and fixed to a certain building belonging to him; and that Hurry had been before convicted of felony.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM SEABORN . I live at Camberwell. I am building twenty houses in Knacker's-lane, Lower-road, Deptford—I was there on the evening of the 27th of March, and left those premises all safe between six and seven o'clock in the evening—ten of the houses were glazed—I went the next morning between six and seven—I found I had lost some glass and frames—I found some of the frames had been taken out of the houses, and put in a field—I fetched them back, and missed seventy-five panes of glass from them—seven sashes had been taken from seven different houses—they were the sashes of the lower back rooms in each bouse—they had all been in their places the night before—the beads were in, but the lines were not—the sashes were kept in by the support of the beads—the frames were left across a garden field, not against the premises—these panes of glass fit exactly into ✗hese squares of the sash—there were six sashes of this size that I lost he✗his frame was one that was carried across the field—window-frames a ✗ sorts of sizes—it is not very usual to see a square of glass fit into a ✗ as this does—you may go to a hundred shops and not find a square of glass to fit this frame.
WILLIAM NOAKES ( police-constable M 104.) On the 27th of March I was on duty at a quarter before nine o'clock in the evening, in Russell-street, Bermondsey, which is a little more than a mile from Mr. Seaborn's, and met the two prisoners, one on each side of the way—they were coming in a direction from Mr. Seaborn's houses, and each carrying a bundle—I heard the glass make a noise—I stopped Clarke, and asked what he had got there—he said, "Glass"—I asked where he brought it from—he said from his master's, in High-street, Deptford—he mentioned some name, which I do not recollect—he then said to Hurry, who was on the other side, "Give this officer one of our cards"—he then said, "Give him one of our governor's cards, it is all right"—Hurry took out a card or a piece of paper, but he did not give it to me, he put it into his pocket again—I then asked them both where they were going to take it to—they said, "To our governor's, in Blackfriars-road"—I took Clarke by the collar, to save the glass from falling—Hurry threw his bundle down, and ran away—on the road to the station Clarke said he did not know anything about the glass; the person who had ran away had given it to him to carry—I noticed a good deal of fresh putty on his hands, and he seemed to wish to get rid of something—I found this knife in his pocket, all over putty—I found about thirty-five panes of glass on Clarke—Hurry's bundle was picked up by a person standing by, at my request—that contained about forty squares of glass, but some of them were broken—I gave directions, and Hurry was taken afterwards.
JOHN WEST ( police-constable M 249. I took Hurry on the 31st of March—I told him he was charged with being with Clarke—he said he did not know Clarke.
WALTER TYLER ( City police-constable, No. 373.) I produce a certificate of Hurry's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace of
Surrey—( read—Convicted 2 nd of July, 1844, and confined Six month)—the prisoner is the person.
CLARKE — GUILTY . Aged 19.
HURRY — GUILTY . Aged 24.
Transported for Seven Years.

1004. JAMES POWER and WILLIAM ABBOTT were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Henry Harris, on the 8th of March, at Camberwell, and stealing therein 1 cloak, value 1 l.; 4 gowns, 1 l. 19 s.; 2 sheets, 16 s.; 1 apron, 1 s.; and 1 pair of shoes, 4 s.; his property.
ANN HARRIS . I am the wife of Henry Harris, of No. 5, Edmond-street, Camberwell. On Saturday night, the 8th of March, I left my house at twenty minutes after eight o'clock, leaving nobody in it—I left it all fastened up—I returned at ten minutes to nine—I found my front gate unbolted, which I had left bolted—all the doors and wint as✗ were fast as I had left them, but when I got in I found a lucifer on ✗ mat—I know nobody could have been in the house when I went out—it is a cottage all on one floor—there are hangings to my bed, but a person could not be concealed there—I went to light my candle, and found it was gone out of the candlestick—I went into my kitchen and found a mahogany caddy laid there broken open—it had been taken from under my bed in my bed-room—my box was open, and four gowns, two shawls, a cloak, an apron, a pair of shoes, and a pair of boots taken out—they had dropped one boot by the bedside—I saw all my property again, except the candle, the same night at the station—it is worth 4 l.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Are there any valens to your bed? A. No—there are curtains—it is a tent bedstead—I have another bed in a little room, but that has no top—there arc sideboard cupboards—I had been in the house all day—I have no servant.
WILLIAM BOWMAN . I live at Walworth, a quarter of a mile from Mr. Harris—on Saturday night, the 8th of March, from a quarter to half-past eight, I saw both the prisoners together, nearly opposite Harris's house—Power went up against the door and came away from it again—I stopped for ten minutes watching them, and they came by me, just before I got to them, Power said to Abbott, "It is no use stopping here any longest is too late now, they won't come yet awhile"—I went into the Rose public-house, and spoke to a young man, then came out, and went up George-street to see for a policeman—I saw the prisoners standing under the window of the Rose—I could not find a policeman—I came back, went into the Rose, and they were over by a shop window—they came acoss again, and stood under the Rose window—I met Davis, a policeman, and told him what I had seen.
Cross-examined. Q. How long did you lose sight of them? A. For an hour, after I came out of the Rose—I went about half-past tea o'clock to Harris's with the policeman—Mrs. Harris was at home then—I had not seen her go out.
Abbott. Q. Can you swear to me? A. swear to both of you.
by your dress and everything—I saw you both in custody at the station that night—you were strangers to me before.
JAMES BAKER ( policeman.) On the 8th of March, from half-past eight to a quarter to nine o'clock, I saw the prisoners together and stopped them—Abbott had got a bundle—I went up to them, and asked Abbott what he had got there—Power answered, "I have got something for my mother," and said, Abbott was carrying it for him—I asked what it was—Power said he had brought it from Mr. Hawkins's, a confectioner, in Camberwell-road—I asked whereabouts—he said the other side of the green—I asked where there—he said leading away to Peckbam—I asked where he was going to carry it—he said to the Elephant and Castle—I asked where there—he said past Horsemonger-lane, the first turning on the right—I asked whether it was the house before he got to Horsemonger-lane—he said, "Yes"—I said there was no house, only a coachmaker's before you come to the lane, and I did not believe his story, I should take them to the station, and inquire whether it was true or not—Power said, "Will you go to the place with me?"—I said I did not believe his statement—he said it was not far, and he was not going to pass the Elephant and Castle, he was going to turn down the New Kent-road—I asked whether he worked for Hawkins—he said he did—I asked for how long—he said for some little time, and it was Mr. Hawkins's brother he was going to take the clothes to—I asked what Hawkins's brother was—he said he could not tell me—I asked the name of the place where he lived—he said he did not know—I called Jones, a constable over, and we took them to the station with the handle—they said at first they would not go with me—we left them in charge of the inspector, and I and Jones went to Camberwell to inquire for Hawkins, but could not find any such person—I saw the bundle opened at the station—it contained the articles stated—the prosecutrix saw and claimed them—Abbott said he was carrying them for Power.
CHARLES JONES ( police-constable P 230.) I assisted in taking the prisoners to the station—as we were going I asked Power where he got the things from—he said he had brought them from Mr. Hawkins, a confectioner at Camberwell, and was going to take them to Mr. Hawkins's brother in Horsemonger-lane—I said it might be true, but I doubted it—he directly answered, "Oh, you are a very nice man; but I do not blame you if you can get a shilling or two by it."
RICHARD DAVIS ( police-constable P 55.) I saw Bowman, and in consequence of what he said to me I went to Edmond-street, made inquiry, and found that Mrs. Harris's house had been robbed—(I had previously seen the prisoners and the bundle at the station)—I saw this boot on the bed at Mrs. Harris's—I said I had every reason to believe that the fellow boot was at the station—I took possession of it—this tea-caddy was broken open.
MRS. HARRIS re-examined. The articles in this bundle are nine, and were in my box when I went out that night.
Cross-examined. Q. When you got home was your door open? A. No, I found it locked as I had left it—I unlocked it—there was no other mode of getting into the house—I have back windows, but they were all fastened—I examined every one when I came home.
COURT. Q. What parish is Camberwell in? A. St. Giles's, Camberwell, it is called—I always understood it to be St. Giles's, Camberwell.
RICHARD DAVIS re-examined. I have been on the heat fifteen years, and always understood it to be St. Giles's, Camberwell, never any other way.
Abbott's Defence. I went into the Walworth-road about seven, o'clock in the evening, and went as far as Camberwell-green, and turned into no turning out of the open road until the policeman took me; I met Power; I had seen him before, but was not in his company much; he asked where I was going; I said, "Home;" I walked with him a good bit; he asked me if I would carry the bundle for him, as his hand was cold; I asked where he was going; he said he had a job to carry it for a gentleman at Camberwell to Horsemonger-lane; I said I would carry it as far as the Elephant and Castle, and then leave him; I carried it until the policeman stopped me; I have been in the habit of going to sea.
(Power received a good character.)
POWER — GUILTY . Aged 19.
ABBOTT — GUILTY . Aged 20.
Of larceny only.— Confined Two Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18450407-1006]
1006. JAMES HERBERT was indicted for feloniously assaulting William Girling on the 27th of March, and cutting and wounding him in and upon his head, with intent to murder him:—2nd COUNT, stating his intent to be to disable:—3rd COUNT, to do some grievous bodily harm.
WILLIAM GIRLING . I am a shoemaker, and live at No. 12, Nelson'splace, Locksfields. The prisoner is married to my niece—he is a shoemaker, and lodged with me up to Thursday, the 27th of March—hs came home about a quarter to two o'clock that day—I think he was in liquor—I went down to his room and asked if he had any rent for me—he owed me 7 s. rent—he said he had none for me—I said it was not likely that he would have any, for he had been out three days previous, and had not been at work, he had been losing his time so, idling about—I told him I would forgive him the rent be owed me if he would go out of my place—he said I could not afford to lose it, and he should not go until he had paid me—I said I thought I was authorised to put his things outside the door—he said he knew the law as well as me, I must not put them out, if I did I should find him a different man to what he was (he had been a very quiet well conducted man before) my wife was there, and portion of his furniture and put it out at the door—I think it was a child's chair, and another chair—when she came in again he went over towards her—I said, "Jem, do not strike my wife"—he had not lifted his hand to strike her at that moment, but I thought he was about to do so—I cannot say whether I pushed a table up against him, or whether I did not, or whether he thought I intended to strike him, but he took up a hammer
and struck me with it on the head—I cannot say that he struck me more than once—it stunned me—I cannot say whether I lost my senses or not, but I heard my wife scream; that brought me to myself—I was bleeding—I went over to Mr. Dry, the surgeon—he dressed my head—I was bleeding all the way I went—I walked home again, and went to bed—I kept my bed for a week, without getting up—I am a great deal better now, quite nicely—it was a shoemaker's hammer he struck me with—it was in the room where he worked, and the hammer laid down before him—I have known him from a boy, I should say twelve or fourteen years—he always behaved very quiet—that was his character up to the time he struck me.
ELEANOR GIRLING . I am the wife of William Girling. I remember the prisoner coming home on Thursday—he has two children—the eldest little girl was in my room when he came home—he spoke from one room to the other to the child, and asked her what business she had with me—I said I took her in out of good nature, as he had left her with a fire in the room—I afterwards went into the room when my husband was there—I began to remove a chair or two from the room—when I came back, I laid my hand on a tea-board—I did not see the prisoner come towards me, but I heard my husband say, "Don't strike my wife!"—I saw my husband standing still—the prisoner took up a hammer, and struck him one blow—I screamed and threw my arms round my husband's neck—he seemed to reel, and put his hand to his head—he did not fall—the prisoner stood still—I took up a little chair, and tried to strike him with it—he threw up the window, and told the people outside what rent he owed—he remained abont the place a little while, and then went away—he said he would not strike me—I could not see whether my husband's head bled, for my sight seemed to leave me, I was so frightened.
GEORGE WESTON ( policeman,) I was sent for to Mr. Dry's about a quarter past two o'clock—I found him dressing Girling's head, which was bleeding very much—I went home with him—I was shown into a shoemaker's room by his wife, and found this hammer up in one corner of the front parlour—there were four or five hairs on the sharp part of it—there was a quantity of blood about the room—the prisoner was not there.
HENRY DRY . I am a surgeon. Girling came to me about two o'clock in the afternoon—he had two incised wounds in the head, over the left eye—the sharp edge of this hammer might have inflicted them—a branch of the temporal artery was severed by it, and a considerable quantity of blood lost—he has been under my care ever since, and is not well yet—I think he will do well—it is impossible to say whether he may not suffer permanently from it—the wound is of that character that weeks and months might elapse before any symptom might present itself—I do not think him out of danger—when before the Magistrate, I called it a dangerous wound—it must have been inflicted by two blows, for the upper wound is longitudinal, and the other transverse.
WILLIAM MORTON ( policeman.) I apprehended the prisoner in a little street leading into Suffolk-street, Borough, on the 27th of March, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening—I told him the charge against him was for assaulting Mr. Girling—he said, "Assaulting! what, in my own place?"—I said, "Yes"—on going to the station, he said, "They cannot hang me up by the heels, and what they give me I shall be able to bear"—next
morning, outside the court, he said he was striking at nis arm, he did not mean to hit his head, but his hammer slipped.
WILLIAM GIRLING re-examined. This hammer belongs to the prisoner—it is the hammer I was struck with—I was not in the room when he came home—I did not see any pudding in the room.
ELEANOR GIRLING re-examined, I did not see any pudding in the room—I could not see whether his hands were greasy.
Prisoner's Defence. I owed Girling 9 s.; he asked me for it; he stid, "Take your things away;" I said, "No, I will not take them away till I have paid you; you cannot afford to lose the rent more than I can;" with that his wife took two chairs, and a tea-board outside the door; he ran In and took the table up, and was in the act of turning it out; I took up the hammer, and was going to strike him on the arm, but it slipped out of my hand.
MR. DRY re-examined. There was an inch between the two wouods, and you never get a longitudinal and a transverse wound at the same time—one must have been done when he was on the right side, and the other when on the left, and he had a contused wound on the right arm, so there must have been three blows—the wound on the arm was fresh.
WILLIAM GIRLING re-examined. I had received no wound on my arm before I entered the room—all the injury was done after I entered the room.
GUILTY on the 3rd COUNT. Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor. — Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Justice Coliman.

1010. JOHN PICKERING was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of April, 1/4 of a yard of lasting, value 8 d.; and 1/4lb weight of leather, 6 d. the goods of Henry Tune, his master.
HENRY TUNE . I am a shoemaker, and live in Blackfriars-road. The prisoner was in my employ for nearly twelve months, and worked on the premises as a clipper—he had no authority to take any work or anything off the premises—on Friday evening, the 4th of April, he left my house about five minutes past eight o'clock, at his usual time—in consequence of suspicion I sent for him to come back, and told him I suspected he had some property on him belonging to me—he at first denied it—I sent for a policeman, and while he was gone for he pulled a piece of leather out of his pocket, with a piece of something else, and said that was all he had got, and he hoped I should forgive him—he placed it upon the table—it was my property—it had my mark upon it—I afterwards saw him searched at the station, and some lasting found on him which he had no authority to take—these are the things produced—I afterwards went to his lodgings and found a great quantity of materials of every description belonging to me—he had not the slightest authority to take them—he had 16 s. a week.
ALEXANDER HAUGHEY ( policeman,) I took the prisoner into custody on the 4th of April—I searched him at the station, and found this lasting on him.
The prisoner put in a petition confessing his guilt, pleading poverty, and imploring mercy for the sake of his wife and children.
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Six Months.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18450407-1011]
1011. WILLIAM BENBOW was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of William Harding Birchall, from his person.
WILLIAM HARDING BIRCHALL . I have an office in Newgate-street. On Sunday evening, the 22nd of March, between six and seven o'clock, I was walking in the London-road—the witness Goldsmid came and touched me on the shoulder—in consequence of what he said, I felt in my outside coat pocket, and missed a silk handkerchief, which I had safe not a minute before—it has never been found.
JOSEPH GOLDSMID . I live at Newington-causeway. On Sunday evening, the 22nd of March, I was in the London-road, and saw two boys—I saw one of them pick the prosecutor's pocket of a handkerchief—I cannot swear it was the prisoner, or that he was one of the two—they ran away—I was on the opposite side of the way—I was examined before the Magistrate—the signature to this deposition is mine—( looking at it.)
Q. Listen to this, "I saw the prisoner take from the prosecutor's hind pocket a blue handkerchief with white spots; I know him by sight quite well; I have been in the habit of seeing the prisoner for the last twelvemonth, and I am quite certain he is the person that took the prosecutor's handkerchief." Witness. My not swearing to him now is in consequence of passing through a field, and observing, among other boys assembled there for the purpose of gambling, a person resembling the prisoner so much, that I cannot swear to him now—when before the Magistrate I laboured under the delusion that it was him; but seeing another lad so like him, I cannot conscientiously swear to him.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450407-1012]
1012. ELIZA JOHNSON was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 guard-chain, 10 s.; 1 watch-key, 1 d.; and 1 handkerchief, 1 s.; the goods of William Bland, from his person.
WILLIAM BLAND . I am in the Royal Artillery. I was at Richmond, recruiting—on the 31st of March, about nine o'clock in the evening, I met the prisoner in the street, and went with her to the Magpie public-house, for about an hour, drinking with her—I had my watch in my watch-pocket—I had not my belt on—I afterwards came out, walked about with her till about twelve o'clock, and then went back with her to the Magpie—she left, and after she was gone I missed my watch—there were other persona in the Magpie besides her—I had taken my watch out, and put it into another pocket by my side—I am sure I had it when I first left the Magpie with her—I had not been in any other house with her—this is my watch.
WILLIAM MILLICHAP ( police-constable V 33.) At a quarter before one o'clock in the morning, on the 1st of April, I met the prosecutor—he was not sober—he gave me information, and I went after the prisoner—I found her in Cleaver-street, Richmond, the next morning—she said she had not been in Richmond the evening before, she had been to Hampton fair, dancing with some soldiers there, and had just returned—when I first saw the prosecutor he was dozing, and when I spoke to him he told me of his loss—his jacket was unbuttoned, and his trowsen a little unbuttoned, and his braces hanging down behind him—I took him home, and put him to bed—he was stupidly drunk.
HENRY HOLLAND ( police-constable V 122.) I saw the prisoner with the prosecutor that night in George-street, Richmond, about 300 yards from the Magpie—I afterwards saw her about a quarter past twelve o'clock—she was running—a man called to her, and she said she could not stop—I afterwards saw the prosecutor coming up an alley, very tipsy—he could scarcely walk—I then met Millichap, and we found the prosecutor sitting down near the road—we lifted him up, and took him home—I do, not believe he knew what he was about.
MARY ANN GRORGE . I searched the prisoner at the station.—I found on her this watch and guard, and silk handkerchief—she said, "That is the watch."
Prisoner's Defence. He said he had spent all his money, and he, left me his watch till the next day; the handkerchief he gave me.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18450407-1013]
1013. JOHN CLARKE was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 4 l. 10 s.; and 1 watch-guard, 10 s.; the goods of William Cresford, from his person.
WILLIAM CRESFORD . I am a ship-carpenter, and live in East-street, Commercial-road. On the 24th of March, about nine o'clock at night. I went into a public-house in the Borough—I called for some gin and water—I was the worse for liquor when I went in—after I had been there a short time I saw the prisoner and others come in front of the bar—I found myself very much the worse for liquor, and I left the house—I had not got twenty yards before some person came and asked me to go back and have something more to drink—I refused—he gave me a shove —I fell, and felt him draw my watch and guard from me—the watch was in my pocket, and the guard over my neck—I got up as well as. I could, and called, "Stop thief!"—I lost sight of the person, but I went on. and met the policeman coming back with the prisoner—I went to the station, and soon after saw my watch and guard brought in by a female—this is it—it
was snatched from me a little after nine, and it was brought to the station between nine and ten, to the best of my recollection.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you recollect whether you went with any woman? A. I have a very imperfect recollection of what happened.
EDWARD OXLEY CLARKE . On the night of the 24th of March I was coming from the Borough-market—I passed St. Thomas-street, about half-past nine o'clock—the prisoner brushed by me, and at the same moment I heard the prosecutor call out, "Stop thief!" and he seemed stumbling—I turned to run after the prisoner—he turned at the back of St. Saviour's church—the policeman, hearing the cry, caught him—I am sure he is the man that I saw running from the prosecutor.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. A shop ticket-writer—I was not examined by the Magistrate, but was subpcenaed here by the policeman—I could not have been above three yards from the prisoner—he ran fast, and was stopped at the corner of Winchester-street—I live at No. 1, Fountain-court, in the Minories.
ELIZABETH MADDERS . I live at No. 6, Winchester-street. About ten o'clock at night, on the 24th of March, I was sitting in the parlour, and heard a noise—I went out, saw the prisoner running, and saw his left hand move at the corner of Winchester-street, just after you pass St. Saviour's church—I went to that spot, at the corner of the street where I saw his hand move, and found this watch—I took it to the station.
Cross-examined. Q. What are you? A. I am single; I live with my father, who is a tinman—when I went out I saw the prisoner in custody of a constable—it was while he and the constable were going along that I saw him make the motion with his hand.
Q. Did you not say you saw him running, and make a motion with bis hand? A. Yes, so I did, but it was after the policeman had got him, and they were going along together—I assist my father in his business.
THOMAS MOORE ( police-sergeant M 11.) I saw the prisoner running in Winchester-street—I took him—Madders brought this watch, which I produce—the prisoner went by the corner of Winchester-street, where this watch was found.
GUILTY.* Aged 20— Transported for Ten Years.

1017. JAMES PARSONS was indicted for stealing 27 deals, value 3 l. 10 s.; and 57 wooden planks, 9 l.; the goods of the Company of proprietors of the Grand Surrey Canal.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Charles Walton and others.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM PETERS . I am in the employ of the Grand Surrey Canal Company. On the 6th of March my attention was called to the deals—there ought to have been forty-six—I found by the book there were fortythree deficient—I did not make the entry in the book, but I saw the deals after they were entered, and I had sold several parcels from that pile—there was a pile of 147, and fifty-five and forty-fix had been delivered—the rest, which had been sold to Mr. Scarf, remained—I saw them safe on the 4th of March, and missed forty-three of them on the 6th—I then went to look at sixteen which I had sold to Mr. Hinton, and out of tkem I missed fourteen—I missed in all fifty-seven planks and forty-three deals, eighty-four pieces in all—I went to Archer's Wharf, and saw a punt lying there, which I had seen the day before four or five ships dowa, and the eighty-three pieces of wood were on the brick-wall—they were the pieces I had seen safe on the premises of the Canal Company on the Tuesday before—I saw the prisoner shortly after, on Mr. Archer's wharf in custody—I said I was sorry to see him there—he said, "I only hired the punt for Johnson"—I knew the prisoner—I cannot tell whether he was employed on the water.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLAMTINE. Q. What did you go to the book for? A. I knew the deals were missing, and I went to see if anybody else had delivered any of them—I could not have ascertained that feet without the book, because I am not the foreman—the delivery-book is not here—Griffiths and three or four others have a right to deliver goods—they take orders, and go and mark off the goods—I have never been what I call suspended—I would rather not mention what it was.
WILLIAM LANGPORD . I am a lighterman. On the 3rd of March the prisoner and Harwood came to me—Harwood said the prisoner wanted a punt—I said mine was stolen, but I could get them one for 3 s.—the prisoner said, "The old man will not mind 3 s."—I showed them Patey's punt, and they said it was too big for them, they wanted one of twelve or fourteen feet for scaffolding boards—they both Baid the little one would do—I took the punt, and put her into the canal, and made her fast—the prisoner said, "Take her below the ships, and I will leave you a pint of ale at the Bull"—he said Harwood would take charge of her—that wat on the Wednesday, and on Thursday I saw Harwood, who came that morning from Blackheath with the old gentleman—he said he had taken a punt from the Commercial Docks with a short 100 of deals in it, and he had turned them into the punt that I took up, I should find the punt at Smith's Lock.
WILLIAM PETBRS re-examined. Q. You say Griffiths and several other persons might deliver deals? A. Yes, but they could not deliver these, because they were stolen—they are not here, and the book is not here.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18450407-1018]
1018. GEORGE FOGG was indicted for embezzlement.
SARAH FARRELL . I deal with Mr. Thomas Yates. On the 28th of March I paid the prisoner 3 s. 6 d. for his master, for meat which I had had at different times.
ANN EVANS . I paid the prisoner, on the 29th of March, 2 l. 4 s. 9 1/2 d. for his master, on account of my mistress—I am quite sure of it.
THOMAS YATES . I am the prisoner's master. On the 28th of March I did not receive 3 s. 6 d. from Sarah Farrell, nor on the 29th of March, 2 l. 4 s. 9 1/2 from Ann Evans—I am quite clear I did not receive either of them—he ought to pay me every day.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How long had he been in your service? A. About a year and a half—he has sometimes paid money to my wife, when I was out of the way—he was taken upon the 2nd of April.
COURT. Q. Have you demanded this money of him? A. No, not till he was given into custody—on the Wednesday morning my little boy brought me a book, which was to be made up and returned—the prisoner told me he had received the money—the book and money had been sent by him on the 29th—this is the book which I sent as a bill—the book was to be returned immediately, and he kept it till the Wednesday morning.
NOT GUILTY .

1019. GEORGE FOGG was again indicted for embezzlement.
JANE TRACEY . I paid the prisoner 10 s. on the 31st of March, for his master for meat.
THOMAS YATES . The prisoner was my servant—I did not receive 10 s. from him on the 31st of March—I never received 10 s. from him on account of Mrs. Tracey—my wife is in the habit of receiving money from the prisoner—on Tuesday morning, the 1st of April, I said to the prisoner, "Ask Mrs. Tracey for money," and he said, "Very well"—my wife is not here—she is not able to attend—no conversation has passed between her and me in the prisoner's presence about this money.
NOT GUILTY .

1021. JOHN BALL was indicted for stealing 1 10 l. bank-note the monies of Henry Flower; and EDWARD LEWIS and SAMUEL LEWIS , for feloniously receiving the same, knowing it to be stolen.
MR. ADOLPHUS conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY FLOWER . I am managing clerk to Messrs, Goding and Co., brewers, in Belvedere-road, Lambeth. Ball was employed about their premises in Nov. last—he was to wait on the clerks, and go on errands or messages for them—in Nov. last I had a 10 l. note of my own, which I put into a drawer in the strong closet in the brewery—Ball bad access to that closet—the key of the drawer was on a bunch of keys which was in the closet—I missed the note on my return from Dover, about the 20th of Nov.—it had been safe two or three days before.
ROBERT FENNING ( police-constable L 181.) On the 24th of Feb. I went to Goding's brewery—I saw Ball—Mr. Goding sent for him to the counting-house—I cautioned him not to say anything, and I said I was to take him for some other things—I named the, 10 l. note—he said he bad not taken it—he afterwards said something, and I fetched Harvey to the station—Harvey said in Ball's presence that Ball had taken a 10 l. note and brought it to Lewis—Ball denied it—Harvey said, "You know you brought the 10 l. note and another note, and received 6 l. for it, you may as well tell the truth, for I shall"—nothing further passed.
JOHN PETER PERRY . I am clerk at Lambeth Police-court. When Ball was brought up for examination he said, ( reading) "What he says (meaning Harvey) is right; they gave me 6 l. for the 10 l. and 5 l. notes, but asked me for 3 l. back, because they said they could not change the 10 l. note."Harvey says," When Ball came for the 6 l. I was there; he told me that he had a 5 l. note and a 10 l. note, and sold them for 6 l."
Ball. James Halnut came up with Harvey, and told me to say what Harvey said, but what Harvey said was not true.
WILLIAM HARVEY . I am fourteen years old. I was in the service of Edward and Samuel Lewis till they were taken into custody—I know Pall quite well—I have seen him at Lewis's lodgings, at No. 3, Artichoke-court, Lambeth—I have seen Ball there in a morning, and the Lewis's were up in a corner whispering about notes, and in going up the Cut one night Ball said to me, "I sold Samuel Lewis a 5 l. note and a 10 l. note for 6 l., and be made me give him 3 l. back, he said there was one note bad, and he could not change it"—I heard the Lewis's say up in the room that Ball had got a 5 l. note and a 10 l. note, and "I will take them to Bill to change them"—Edward and Samuel Lewis both said that—Samuel Lewis sent me one night to Ball to get some money—he told me to tell Bali that Edward was ill, and he wanted some money—Ball told me he got the notes from Mr. Goding's brewery, and another thing he told me that he took a check off Mr. Goding's table, and he said it was a g-s shame that the Lewis only gave him 6 l. for the notes, and made him give them 3 l. back.
Samuel Lewis. Q. Did you ever see Ball give me a 5 l. note? A. No, but you and Edward Lewis went up in a corner and whispered about notes—I used to see Ball at your place very often—you was not at work at Mr. Starling's when Ball came up—you did not work at Mr. Starling's while I was with you, which was eight, or nine, or ten week-Ball tow me he brought the notes one day—you, and I, and Edward Lewis were there when Ball came up—you called him in the corner, and whispered to
him—we had not long got up—he brought a basket, and put it down by the window.
Samuel Lewis. I never had the notes—I worked at Mr. Starling's from the 11th of Nov. till after Christmas—my brother told me that Harvey had been at Union-hall. Witness. I never was taken up in my life for anything.
BALL — GUILTY. Aged 12.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three
Months.
EDWARD LEWIS — GUILTY . Aged 12.— Confined Three Months.
SAMUEL LEWIS — GUILTY . Aged 18.—( See the next case.)

1022. WILLIAM HENRY WARREN was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of Feb., 12ozs. weight of India-rubber, value 1 s. the goods of Charles Kay, and another, his masters; and the said SAMUEL LEWIS and EDWARD LEWIS for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
BENJAMIN NICHOLLS . I live in York-street, Lambeth, and am manager to Charles Kay and another—Warren had been in their employ, and was discharged—they have lost some India-rubber—I have examined that which is produced, and I believe it belongs to my masters—there is no mark on this, but in the place where Warren worked, there were 300 or 400 of such pieces, and he had an opportunity of taking it.
WILLIAM HARVEY . I was living with the Lewis's, in Artichoke-court—I know Warren by his coming to dine in their room, petty nearly every day—he used to have it brought to him ready cooked by his mother—there was only myself living with the Lewis's—I had my lodging there—one Thursday at dinner-time Warren brought up an India-rubber bottle, and gave it to Samuel Lewis, who looked at it, and said, "Very well," and chucked it under the bed—I believe Edward Lewis was out—I only saw Warren bring one piece of rubber.
CHARLES STEWART ( police-constable L 185.) On the 22nd of Feb. I went to Lewis's lodgings—Fenning found one of these bottles of rubber under the bed, and the other in the box.
ROBERT FENNING ( police-constable L 181.) I found this bottle of rubber under the bed, and this other in the box—I took Warren—I told him I wanted him for some India-rubber—he said it was all right, his father had seen. Mr. Nicholls, and settled it—I said, no, he had not, for I had just come from there—he then said he had taken one, and no more.
Warren. I did not say so.
WARREN — GUILTY. Aged 15.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
SAMUEL LEWIS — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
EDWARD LEWIS — NOT GUILTY .

1025. WILLIAM HEATH and ALFRED WRIGHT were indicted for stealing 80 lbs. weight of coals, value 1 s., the goods of Henry Philip Davis; and that Heath had been before convicted of felony.

JAMES FROST ( police-constable L 59.) I was on duty about eight o'clock in the evening, on the 25th of March, in Palace-yard, Lambeth—I saw the prisoner by the side of Mr. Davis's barge—I saw Heath lift Wright into the barge, and Wright rolled some coals to the side, he gave them to Heath, who took them, and they were removed fifteen of sixteen yards from the barge.

Wright. I was not near the barge.
